A 1,000-mile cloud on Mars may reveal physics never seen in nature
2026-10-24

A 1,000-mile cloud on Mars may reveal physics never seen in nature

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-24

Visit Original Website
Every morning during the Martian spring and summer, a thin and extremely long cloud emerges, only to disappear again. The wispy formation appears near the volcano Arsia Mons and gradually elongates until it reaches over 1,000 miles—about the distance from New York to Kansas City as the crow flies—before dissipating. This cycle then repeats for several months. Since it was first recorded by a probe in 2018, scientists have been unable to find a plausible explanation for the existence of this cloud, often referred to as the Arsia Mons Elongated Cloud (AMEC). Until now. Recently, researchers successfully modeled the cloud’s formation for the first time, their results suggesting that it may be driven by a physical process that has never been directly observed in any planet’s atmosphere. “We found that we needed to include some exotic physics… physics that, while included in textbooks, is treated as theoretical and usually thought not to happen in nature. It certainly hasn’t been seen in action before,” Jorge Hernández-Bernal, a planetary scientist at Paris’ Sorbonne Université and lead author of the study, said in a press release. “Once we included this physics in our simulations, the AMEC emerged just as we hoped.” On Earth, clouds usually form when water vapor creates droplets or ice crystals in the presence of tiny particles, such as dust or salt. The new study, published in Nature Geoscience, instead suggests AMEC could be forming through so-called homogeneous nucleation, where vapor changes directly into ice crystals on its own without any help from debris. “It’s akin to droplets of condensation appearing in the middle of a room, rather than on a window,” Hernández-Bernal explained. “It’s wholly unexpected.” This phenomenon requires extreme conditions—a sharp temperature drop of 30° Celsius within 10 minutes and high relative humidity. The Arsia Mons volcano also plays a key role. When wind current hits the 65,000-foot-tall volcano, it creates a powerful wave that launches moist air high in the atmosphere in a matter of minutes. Scientists have assumed a similar phenomenon could occur in the upper atmospheres of other planets, including Venus and even Earth, but it has never been observed. That’s not too surprising given the extreme air humidity required for this phenomenon, which is more than 100,000 times greater than any value measured on Earth. Before this modeling, it was not known that these humidity levels existed on Mars either. “We don’t have nearly as much information about Mars’ atmosphere as we do about Earth’s, so reproducing the AMEC to this degree is a big success,” said Hernández-Bernal, adding, “the result is remarkable.” This story originally appeared on WIRED Czech Republic and Slovakia and has been translated from Czech.
Ars Technica - All content
Ice sheet expansion may have started 2,000 years ago, but we canceled that
2026-10-24

Ice sheet expansion may have started 2,000 years ago, but we canceled that

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-24

Visit Original Website
Barnes Ice Cap sits on the central plateau of Baffin Island, in the Canadian Arctic. It is the last surviving fragment of the Laurentide Ice Sheet, the largest of the Pleistocene ice sheets, which at its peak buried most of Canada under two miles of ice and locked away around 80 meters of sea level. That remnant has now yielded direct physical evidence that ice sheets might have reversed their retreat and started to grow again thousands of years ago. In a recent Science study, researchers show that the Laurentide reached its smallest size about 3,600 years ago, then started re-expanding roughly 2,000 years ago, which the team interprets as the opening move of the next glaciation. Apparently, this brand-new ice age was on its way until about 1960, when human-caused climate warming reversed it. “Barnes is the last remaining piece of one of the domes of the Laurentide Ice Sheet,” says Nicolás Young, a geologist at Columbia University’s Lamont-Doherty Earth Observatory and lead author of the study. As that enormous dome thinned through the Holocene, its margin withdrew up onto Baffin Island, and what’s left today is the deflated core of something that was once two miles thick. The reason Barnes can testify about its own history, Young explains, comes down to the temperature at its base. Most glaciers are warm-based, meaning meltwater at the bed lets them slide, and sliding ice grinds up everything beneath it, erasing the record. Barnes is cold-based, and thus frozen directly onto the bedrock beneath it, which prevents it from sliding. “If there is an ice event where you live and you have to go and scrape off your windshield, that ice that’s plastered to the windshield of your car, that’s a good example of how cold-based ice operates. It’s sort of just stuck to what it’s on,” Young says. This stuck ice preserves what’s beneath instead of eroding it. As the Laurentide retreated across the plateau, tundra plants colonized the newly bare ground. When the ice came back later, it simply smothered them in place and held them there, intact, for two millennia. To pinpoint the exact moment of this turnaround, Young’s team used two independent dating methods. The first method measures beryllium-10, an isotope that accumulates in the top few centimeters of rock that is exposed to the sky. By sampling a boulder’s upper surface and counting the beryllium, researchers could learn how long it has been uncovered. That technique put a date on the ice sheet’s retreat. It turned out the Laurentide had withdrawn to within today’s ice margin by 3,600 years ago, probably shrinking further by around 3,000. The second dating method looked at the dead plants. “There were all these plants growing, just like today. And included in that are mosses, these little mosses that are rooted in the ground,” Young says. “So they’re there, they’re living their life, and eventually the Laurentide Ice Sheet starts to re-expand. And when that ice margin covers the moss, it literally kills it.” By radiocarbon dating the remnants of the moss, the team could pinpoint the moment it died, yielding what glaciologists call a kill date—the moment the ice arrived at that spot. By stringing multiple kill dates of moss samples taken from different spots, scientists reconstructed a map of an advancing ice margin. About 70 percent of the team’s radiocarbon dates cluster around 1800 CE, at the culmination of the Little Ice Age. “It’s all sort of the same thing,” he says. The Little Ice Age marks the point where Northern Hemisphere glaciers were at their most extensive in recent millennia. At Barnes, Young argues, it was just one late pulse within an advance that had been running for two thousand years. The most important plants for Young’s team, however, are still hidden under ice. Barnes’ margin has retreated 200 to 800 meters since 1960, exposing mosses killed from about 1000 CE onward. Anything older remains buried. To pinpoint the exact moment the ice started advancing, the team had to look at Barnes’ neighbors. Scattered across the same plateau are other small ice caps, close enough to share the same climate. Earlier studies collected nearly 200 kill dates from beneath them—not one is older than 2,000 years. “What that means is that all of those ice caps up there didn’t start advancing or re-expanding until about 2,000 years ago,” Young says. “Since the Barnes Ice Cap is right next door, it’s probably a good bet that it also started expanding 2,000 years ago.” Young argues that continued retreat of the Barnes Ice Cap should eventually reveal the decisive samples. “If we went back there in 20 years or something, as the ice margin has retreated a little more, we actually might be able to sample those 2,000-year-old plants,” he says. According to the study, the ice started re-expanding at this precise moment because two global forces were pulling it in opposite directions, and one of them eventually won. Solar energy reaching high northern latitudes peaked about 10,000 to 11,000 years ago and has declined ever since, pushing the planet toward the next ice age. Meanwhile, atmospheric carbon dioxide crept up by roughly 20 parts per million between 8,000 and 2,000 years ago, increasing the greenhouse effect that was heating the atmosphere up. “That drop in insolation and temperatures actually overwhelmed the small temperature forcing the little bit of carbon dioxide rise over the same time span,” Young explains. Eventually, 2,000 years ago, temperatures dropped far enough due to lower sunlight in the high latitudes, and the effects of carbon dioxide weren’t high enough, so ice sheets started to re-expand. This happened despite the fact that calculations of orbital forcings, together with modeling of how carbon dioxide interferes with glacial inception, have suggested the next ice age was postponed by tens of thousands of years. Young argues there’s really no contradiction between these models and the evidence his team collected in the field. “A lot of people have defined glacial inception as glaciers and ice sheets needing to be of a certain size—big enough that we’ll say, ah, yes, we are into the next ice age,” Young says. “And that’s perfectly reasonable, because until now there’s been no way to actually pinpoint when an ice sheet first turns around and starts to expand.” But a few geological moments after the Laurentide sheet started re-expanding, humanity entered the chat. Greenhouse gases had risen by about 12 ppm between 1750 and 1850, another 50 ppm by 1960, and a further 138 ppm by 2024. “Once we hit the Industrial Revolution, we’re now pumping way more carbon dioxide at a magnitude that has never occurred in the geologic record,” Young says. By 1960, Barnes’ mass balance went negative and its advance ended. Today, Barnes Ice Cap is dying. In the 1960s and ’70s, roughly 35 percent of its surface sat above the area’s snow line. By the early 2000s, about 3 percent did. Today, none of it does. “This means in the summer, it’s all melting. There’s no more accumulation zone. There’s no more new ice forming,” Young says. “An ice cap or glacier cannot survive if it does not have an accumulation zone.” According to his models, under any plausible emissions scenario, Barnes will disappear within a few centuries. So did we actually dodge the bullet by warming the climate so quickly? “Frankly, in modern times, no one wants to live in an ice age climate. Things would be quite hard and quite different,” Young says. He argues, though, that we did not exactly do ourselves a favor here. To get to a true, full ice age climate with big ice sheets, it would have taken several tens of thousands of years. “I would argue that the pumping of greenhouse gases into the atmosphere hasn’t really saved us from anything,” Young claims. What we have done instead is interrupt a cycle that was very old and very regular. “The pacing of ice ages over the last 2.5 million years has been one of the most consistent cycles in the Earth system that we have,” Young says. “We have evidence now that humans, and specifically greenhouse gases, have actually terminated one of the most consistent cycles on Earth.” The study is published in Science: https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aed3379

NASA updates lander plans for Moon landings, finally solves long-festering Orion issue
2026-10-23

NASA updates lander plans for Moon landings, finally solves long-festering Orion issue

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
During a lengthy news conference on Friday, NASA provided a detailed overview of how it plans to return astronauts to the lunar surface as soon as 2028 as part of its Artemis Program. Although much of the information behind these plans has been previously reported at Ars Technica and elsewhere, this marked the first time NASA has laid them out in detail and acknowledged some substantial changes in the architecture. Most notably, for SpaceX’s Starship vehicle, the revised plan calls for the Orion spacecraft to dock with the Starship lunar lander in high-Earth orbit before transiting to the Moon. And for Blue Origin, the mission architecture includes four launches of the New Glenn rocket, but no spacecraft-to-spacecraft refuelings. Additionally, the Blue Origin plan allows for the possibility of its Blue Moon lander rendezvousing with Orion in low-lunar orbit. During the presentation on Friday, NASA officials said they continue to anticipate launching the Artemis III mission—a demonstration involving docking with both Blue Moon and Starship near Earth—by “summer” 2027. And they are still tracking toward a 2028 launch of Artemis IV, which would involve an actual landing on the lunar surface. “This is an achievable path we are on right now,” NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman said. “We have the mandate, we have the resources, and the support from our stakeholders to be able to execute, and that’s what we intend to do.” It is commendable that Isaacman and his leadership have taken a hard look at every element of the Artemis program and sought to make changes where they can to help the lunar lander companies execute and accelerate their plans. But can they hold to those dates? The biggest ask SpaceX engineers had in meetings with NASA in early 2026 was removing the requirement to dock with Orion in a near-rectilinear halo orbit around the Moon. This required more energy and had other difficulties associated with it. When NASA canceled the Lunar Gateway earlier this year, it solved this problem for SpaceX. Now SpaceX will fuel a Starship lunar lander in low-Earth orbit before docking with Orion in high-Earth orbit. The crew will fly to the Moon inside Orion before two of four astronauts transfer into Starship for a landing on the Moon. A key question surrounding SpaceX’s architecture is the number of refuelings required to top off a Starship for a lunar mission. On Thursday a NASA Safety Panel reported that the current number of tanker launches is “about 22.” SpaceX founder Elon Musk said that estimate was wrong and that the actual number will be six to eight. During a question-and-answer session on Friday afternoon, NASA officials declined to put a precise number on the refueling launches required. A lot of uncertainty remains in the lift capacity of Starship, as well as in how much cryogenic methane and liquid oxygen will boil off in orbit and how efficient the process of transferring propellants from one ship to another will be. However, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman said this of the estimate of six to eight launches proffered by Musk: it was possible but represented the “ultimate potential end state” of Starship. In response to NASA’s call for a faster approach to reaching the Moon, Blue Origin has modified its plans such that it would no longer require refueling between vehicles for initial missions. Under the concept of operations released Friday, a New Glenn rocket (this could be an expendable version of the smaller 7×2 variant of New Glenn, or a reusable 9×4 rocket) would launch three “transfer stages” to low-Earth orbit. These transfer stages are modeled after the Blue Moon Mark 1 lander but have some significant modifications. Then New Glenn would launch the Blue Moon Mark 2 lander into orbit, where it would rendezvous with the transfer stages that will push the stack to the Moon. This is not new information, as Ars first reported this architecture in March. However, NASA did reveal a potentially significant change on Friday. The chart shared by NASA showed Orion docking with the Blue Moon lander in an elliptical lunar orbit, approximately 8,500 km above the Moon’s surface. However NASA is also working to have the option of docking with Orion in low-lunar orbit. According to Jeremy Parsons, the manager of the Artemis program for NASA, such an orbit a few hundred km above the Moon would significantly reduce the propellant needed by Blue Moon to bring astronauts down to the lunar surface and back up. This mass could be used elsewhere on the vehicle. The lower orbit would also offer more and faster opportunities for astronauts to get off the lunar surface if needed. In short, it makes some aspects of a lunar landing significantly easier for Blue Origin. As part of this, NASA announced Friday that it is working with United Launch Alliance to develop an “extended” version of the Centaur V upper stage. In concert with the core stage of the Space Launch System, this upper stage would allow Orion to reach low-lunar orbit. This is a big deal. It has long been a point of embarrassment for NASA that Orion, with its low-performing Service Module, does not have the delta-V capability to launch from Earth, reach low-lunar orbit, and return to Earth for splashdown in the Pacific Ocean. But no one had seemed prepared to fix this problem before. “Getting Orion to low-lunar orbit means solving propulsion and spacecraft integration problems that were left unaddressed for far too long,” Isaacman said Friday. Instead, NASA created workarounds like the Lunar Gateway, which was put in a near-rectilinear halo orbit that Orion could safely reach. The burden of performance to reach the lunar surface was pushed on to the lunar landers and the companies developing them. Given the urgency of the situation, NASA has been trying to help lunar lander companies in any way they can, Isaacman said. “We’re listening to the data, and looking at what’s happening around us,” he said. “What progress are our landing partners making? Where are they struggling? What can we do to be helpful? Their lives get easier if we can get Orion closer to the Moon, substantially. We could choose to continue on our track and say, maybe they’ll show up and meet our timelines. Or we can say, well let’s start turning those knobs to help.”

Hegseth warns prediction market bets warp military judgment, endanger troops
2026-10-23

Hegseth warns prediction market bets warp military judgment, endanger troops

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth issued a memo warning military personnel against using nonpublic information to make bets on prediction markets, saying the wagers could give important clues to enemy countries. “Do Not Compromise Military Operations for Personal Financial Gain,” said the subject line of an October 20 memo published yesterday by CBS News. Hegseth’s memo was sent to senior Pentagon leadership, Combatant Command leaders, and Defense Department field activity directors. Our enemies watch movements in these markets. When an American Service member, intelligence professional, or other Department civilian engages in a financial transaction on a military outcome, they create a signal. That signal can be aggregated, correlated, and exploited by hostile foreign intelligence services to anticipate our movements, targets, and timing. The lives of our Service members often depend on the enemy not knowing what is coming. The men and women who carry our flag into harm’s way deserve a Force that protects them—not one that exposes them to the enemy for personal gain. In April, a US Army soldier was arrested and charged with using confidential information to make $410,000 in Polymarket bets on the timing of the military’s capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro. An August report by the Anti-Corruption Data Collective said the group found 152 suspicious wallets that placed longshot bets on military and defense markets and won a combined $8 million. Hegseth’s memo said the prohibition applies to “all financial platforms,” including traditional betting services and prediction markets such as Polymarket and Kalshi. Defense Department personnel may not make any financial transaction informed by nonpublic information, regardless of whether that information is classified or unclassified, Hegseth wrote. “All Department of War personnel are prohibited from engaging in financial transactions using nonpublic information, either directly or indirectly through a third party… Violations may result in administrative, disciplinary, and, where appropriate, criminal action under the Uniform Code of Military Justice and/or applicable federal law,” Hegseth wrote. The memo said betting on military matters related to a person’s job can warp one’s judgment. “Beyond operational security, financial stakes can warp judgment,” he wrote. “A logistician with money riding on a strike date has an interest in that date. An analyst with a position on an outcome has an interest in that outcome.” Kalshi and Polymarket both prohibit insider trading. But criminal charges of the sort described by Hegseth are sure to be a bigger deterrent than being banned from the Kalshi or Polymarket platforms. This was not the first warning issued internally by the Trump administration, The Associated Press wrote yesterday. “A group of new accounts on Polymarket made highly specific, well-timed bets on whether the US and Iran would reach a ceasefire just before President Donald Trump announced it in April, resulting in hundreds of thousands of dollars in profits for the new customers,” the AP wrote. “The White House warned staff against using private information to trade on prediction markets the same day the report came out.” In another incident, a Trump teleprompter aide reportedly made $100,000 on Kalshi by betting on what Trump would say in prepared remarks. Prediction markets have flourished under the Trump administration’s light-touch approach to regulation. The Trump administration is also trying to stop state governments from regulating the markets, arguing that state laws are preempted by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission’s exclusive jurisdiction. Courts have reached different conclusions, and state governments want the Supreme Court to uphold their right to impose restrictions on prediction-market gambling.
Ars Technica - All content
Updated Google privacy policy says your Gmail might be used for advertising
2026-10-23

Updated Google privacy policy says your Gmail might be used for advertising

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
Using Google’s products and services has always been fundamentally precarious—doing so gives the world’s largest advertising company a lot of juicy personal data. Google has long said it won’t use the sensitive data from products like Gmail, Drive, and Photos for ad personalization, but a change to the company’s privacy policy appears to dismantle that firewall. On October 16, Google issued an update to its privacy policy, spotted by Android Authority. The ad personalization section used to include a clear limit on data from certain apps. “We don’t show you personalized ads based on your content from Drive, Gmail, or Photos,” the document said. Now, Google has updated it with the following language. This equivocation opens the door to using data from apps like Gmail and Photos in ads when it’s shared with other Google services, and it just so happens Google has a mechanism for doing that, with Personal Intelligence. The company released Personal Intelligence in early 2026, allowing users to hand data from multiple apps to Google AI products. The idea is that the added context from your existing data will lead to better AI answers, but it’s also proving to be a privacy minefield. Personal Intelligence connects your data to the Gemini app but also to features like AI Mode search. While the Gemini app does not currently have ads, you may see sponsored content in AI search tools. Google’s changes to the privacy policy appear to acknowledge that data shared with AI search via Personal Intelligence could, therefore, contribute to ad personalization. According to Google, this clarification still doesn’t mean ads are “based directly” on your personal data. Here’s the company’s full statement: “Connecting your apps is entirely opt-in. Ads will continue to be relevant to things like your query, the context of the response and your interests. Ads will not be based directly on your Gmail inbox or Photos library, and as always, sensitive information will not be used to tailor ads.” Google’s statement is essentially a roundabout way of confirming that Workspace and Photos data may inform ads. If you interact with AI Mode while Personal Intelligence is enabled, there’s a chance that summaries and insights from your data will inform the answers you get. Because that same data is used for ad targeting, it’s accurate to say that your personal data is an element of ad targeting in AI search. The privacy policy now makes that clear. A Google spokesperson tells Ars that its privacy clarification does not indicate a change in how it handles your data with respect to its advertising partners. It’s not scanning your entire inbox to enhance your advertising profile, and it’s not giving any of this data to third parties. But if you pipe personal data into an AI search tool, the ads it shows you could be based on that information. The core of the problem is that Google wants its products and services to talk to each other. Google sees that wealth of data as a key strength of its AI efforts. Some apps like Gmail are still given protected status in Google’s data handling, but there are now more avenues for that data to get out. The more data you share across Google’s ecosystem, the greater the risk to your privacy. We’ve seen a similar exception in the way Google uses your AI interactions for model training. While Google says it doesn’t use personal data for AI training, it does use your inputs and the model outputs for that purpose. With Gemini integrated with so many services and connected to them via Personal Intelligence, those outputs may very well contain elements of your personal information. Google has said it tries to filter that content from the user activity controls, therefore keeping it out of training sets. However, it doesn’t promise it will always be successful. Google is correct that Personal Intelligence is opt-in, but the company uses various prompts and pop-ups to entice users to turn it on without fully explaining the consequences. You may have turned it on without realizing. If you want to ensure ads never use your personal data in this way, you’ll need to make sure Personal Intelligence is turned off. The easiest way is to access the Gemini settings for Personal Intelligence and disable the connected apps. These settings are also a few levels deep in your main Google account settings under Data & privacy > Personalization settings > Manage Personalized Recommendations in Search Services > Connected Content Apps. From there, you can disable connections to Workspace and Google Photos.
Ars Technica - All content
Inside the Pentagon, the pressure is on to find a home for a new spaceport
2026-10-23

Inside the Pentagon, the pressure is on to find a home for a new spaceport

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
Leaders in the US Space Force would like to have a new spaceport up and running in four years, adding a third heavy-lift launch site to relieve pressure from bases in Florida and California that have shot rockets into orbit for nearly 70 years. The obvious problem with that ambition is 2030 is four years away, and Pentagon officials have not determined where the new spaceport should go or how to acquire land for it. Air Force Secretary Troy Meink first mentioned the service’s search for a third launch base in May. A congressionally mandated study of the Space Force’s launch infrastructure identified the need for “another site that’s capable of heavy and super heavy launch capability,” Meink said in a House Armed Services Committee hearing. The Trump administration released a space transportation policy in August that endorsed the construction of more launch and reentry sites for commercial and military missions. This week, the Space Force general responsible for procuring launch services for the military laid out an “aspirational” timeline for a new launch site. “We need this capability by 2030, but I’m also a pragmatist,” said Lt. Gen. Philip Garrant, head of Space Systems Command. “That is not an inconsequential demand to build a spaceport when you factor in orbital regimes, safety, access to commodities, the Americans that live near it. In many ways, it’s much like an airport, but even louder. So there’s a lot of consideration.” But there’s no firm plan yet for when the military might get moving on actually setting up a new launch base. “It’s going to take a strong partnership with local and state governments,” Garrant said. “As far as decision timelines, we don’t have anything yet.” There were 175 orbital launch attempts last year from Cape Canaveral Space Force Station and Kennedy Space Center in Florida and Vandenberg Space Force Base in California. Space Force officials project the country’s established spaceports will need to support 700 launches per year by 2036. A third launch site would offload some of the launch demand from limited real estate at Cape Canaveral and Vandenberg. It would also provide what Space Force leaders call “geographic resiliency” to mitigate the risk of a natural disaster or military attack knocking the Cape or Vandenberg offline. The federal government already owns a third spaceport at NASA’s Wallops Flight Facility in Virginia, used for suborbital rocket tests and small- and medium-class satellite launches. Small rockets can also launch from Pacific Spaceport Complex, owned by the state of Alaska. There are also commercial options that might be available for a new military spaceport. Ars asked Garrant if the Space Force might consider leasing property from a privately owned launch base, such as the expansive spaceport SpaceX plans to build in southern Louisiana. “We’ve discussed that we need to consider all of those opportunities: public-private partnership, new federal installation, state land, leveraging commercial land—all of those are possibilities. They all have pros and cons. We haven’t made that decision yet, but we’re not discounting any of those options.”

Traders are looking on the bright side despite last week’s carnage in tech stocks
2026-10-26

Traders are looking on the bright side despite last week’s carnage in tech stocks

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Traders are looking on the bright side today: Talks between Saudi Arabia and Iran are ongoing, and the credit ratings agency Moody’s left France’s credit rating unchanged from before the riots. The yield on France’s 10-year bonds eased back below the headline-inducing 5% level to 4.94%; similarly, the U.S. 10-year was at 4.99%. U.S. futures were up this morning prior to the bell in New York after stocks rose in Europe in early trading. Asia was flat to down today as the price of oil remained painfully high—above $114 per barrel. Incoming: Alphabet and Microsoft report Q3 earnings on Wednesday and Amazon reports on Thursday. The Fed decides on the base interest rate on Wednesday—futures markets are it will be held at 3.75%. Last Thursday was a very good day for U.S. stocks, as 337 of the S&P 500 stocks rose on the day. Although you might not have noticed that, given the S&P 500 lost 1.21% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq fell 2.9%. In reality, those declines reflected the overweight status of tech stocks in the index: The equal-weight version of the S&P actually went up. On 2,887 of 7,827 trading days since 1996, 300 or more of the S&P stocks have risen. But last Thursday’s aggregate performance was the second-worst of all those 300-plus days, according to Deutsche Bank’s Jim Reid. “The only worse occasion was 27 January 2025, when the index fell -1.46% despite 351 stocks rising. That was the day DeepSeek challenged assumptions around the need for costly AI capex. NVIDIA fell -17%,” Reid told clients. While stock traders spent much of last week worrying that AI was a bubble, AI executives were worrying that the opposite is true: Their growth is being constrained by a lack of money. Meta’s stock sold off after its earnings call because the company reported its AI capex was so large it now had negative free cash flow, and it raised its guidance on how much capex it was going to spend through the rest of the year. Meta’s 10-Q states it may not be able to raise more money: “Such financings may not be available on favorable terms or at all.” With multiple companies like Oracle, OpenAI, Alphabet and SpaceX trying to raise hundreds of billions through equity or debt, competition for available capital is fierce. “Investors interpreted the move as a sign that even hyperscalers may be rushing to secure financing before competitors absorb the available capital,” according to Ed Yardeni and Elias Griepentrog of Yardeni Research. “Does enough capital even exist to finance everyone’s ambitious AI spending plans?” Broadcom CEO Hock Tan, BlackRock CEO Larry Fink, and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang have all warned recently that there might be enough money to go round, Yardeni said. This chart from Michael Hartnett and his team at Bank of America shows the concentration of bubble stocks inside the overall indexes of their day. With the exception of the railroad bubble of the 1800s, once the market cap of the bubble stocks reaches 40% of the broader index, a crash soon follows, history shows. For long-term investors—people saving into a pension or 401(k)—exchange-traded funds are usually the best idea. Most stock-pickers fail to beat the market, so you might as well own the whole market, which generally puts on 6-10% per year. “Don’t look for the needle in the haystack. Just buy the haystack!” as the late Vanguard founder Jack Bogle used to say. But some aggressive retail investors want the diversification of an ETF with the gains of a day trader. They use leveraged ETFs, which use borrowed money to magnify your gains and losses. Wells Fargo tracked 546 leveraged ETFs in the U.S., with $160 billion in assets between them, and found that levered-up retail buyers love bad news and consistently buy the dip. Last week, they waded into the carnage, which was as bad as last year’s “Liberation Day” selloff as far as they were concerned. Read the full digital edition of today’s Fortune 500 Digest here. How they fumbled the cola wars: Pepsi generates twice the revenue of Coca-Cola, yet its market cap is only half as big
2026-10-26

How they fumbled the cola wars: Pepsi generates twice the revenue of Coca-Cola, yet its market cap is only half as big

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
PepsiCo has suffered many setbacks in recent years, but perhaps few were as symbolic as being jilted by a long-time partner it used to own, KFC. The fried chicken chain said earlier this month that it would begin replacing PepsiCo’s soft drinks with Coca-Cola products at its 3,700 U.S. restaurants in January. The two companies’ partnership stretches back 40 years, to when PepsiCo bought the fast-food chain. (PepsiCo spun KFC off in 1997.) In the 1980s and ’90s, Pepsi waged the cola culture wars with the Pepsi Challenge and TV commercials featuring Michael Jackson and Cindy Crawford. But last year, Pepsi-Cola only had 8.1% of the U.S. carbonated soft drinks market by volume, far behind Coke’s 18.5%. Pepsi is now the third most popular drink behind Dr. Pepper. Coke reshaped its business around brand building and innovation. PepsiCo, meanwhile, remained a sprawling portfolio company where soda was merely one priority among many. The result is a PepsiCo that generates more than twice as much revenue as Coca-Cola, yet its market valuation is only about half as big. Most stock-pickers fail to beat the market, so you might as well own the whole market, which generally puts on 6-10% per year. “Don’t look for the needle in the haystack. AI agents are shopping like never before—and tools like Mastercard’s ‘Pay for Machines’ could turbocharge the trend
2026-10-26

AI agents are shopping like never before—and tools like Mastercard’s ‘Pay for Machines’ could turbocharge the trend

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
It’s been a busy few weeks for AI agents. They’ve been rummaging around bank accounts, hacking crypto wallets and, according to MIT researchers, using their spare time to play. The agents have also been on an unprecedented shopping spree. We’ve known for some time that so-called agentic commerce would become a thing—but that moment is arriving far sooner, and on a scale that few anticipated. “What we’re witnessing is a bit of a turning point. We think it’s accelerating…We’re rapidly getting to something that will see real adoption,” Mastercard’s Head of Product, Jorn Lambert, told me last week. According to Lambert, this lift-off is due to the sudden popularity of mass-market personal agents. He explained that, a year ago, he and other industry leaders believed that MCP and ACP—agent-to-agent communication protocols that require a degree of technical skill—would be the pillars for agentic commerce, but that this assumption has already become outdated. Instead, the adoption curve has been flattened thanks to products like Muse from Meta and OpenAI’s Dots. The upshot is there is now a critical mass of consumers eager to send their agents to the digital mall. To ensure they have something to buy, Mastercard has rolled out a tool called “Agent Pay” that makes it easy for Muse and his friends to carry out transactions not just with retailers, but also government services, including parking tickets. In another intriguing move, Mastercard last week revealed a tool that lets small-business users spin up ad campaigns. I’m not in the habit of plugging credit card companies but, if you have a moment, check out this YouTube video that shows the maker of some sort of granola bar giving her agent a $400 budget to build and execute a social media campaign. This budget covers not just paying TikTok or Instagram, but also acquiring images, music, a script, and other creative elements—a process that would have once taken hours of the granola bar monger’s time, but can now be dispatched in minutes. A significant feature of Mastercard’s tool (Visa and AmEx are presumably building similar products) is it’s all built on x402, a payments protocol that was baked into the original specs for the internet, but never got built until Coinbase resurrected it a few years ago. As Mastercard’s Lambert explained to me, x402 is basically a messaging language that can speak credit card or debit card or stablecoin—or whatever form of payment both parties to the transaction agree upon. Best of all, x402 is open source, meaning anyone can use or build upon it. Another significant element of Agent Pay is that it opens the door to micropayments—a term that describes paying a trifling amount like 27¢ or $1.87 online. Until now, digital merchants and financial networks have shunned such payments as impractical, due in part to processing costs. But now, with stablecoins and agents who don’t mind haggling over whether a piece of data should cost 50¢ or 75¢, we’re likely to see more à la carte micro-offerings in place of every webpage making an irritating demand to buy a monthly subscription to access content. It’s always perilous trying to predict how new technology will be adopted, since our instinct is often to use the new tech—in this case AI agents—to do what we’ve always done before. A classic example is when newspaper publishers treated the internet as a place to post PDF copies of their paper. In the case of agents, what’s likely to happen is that a whole range of unforeseen use cases will emerge that change how we shop and design businesses in the first place. Indeed, Mastercard’s Lambert predicts that tools like Agent Pay will spur a wealth of new businesses founded by college kids tinkering in their dorm rooms. Bring it on. PepsiCo started losing the cola wars when its CEOs lost focus
2026-10-26

PepsiCo started losing the cola wars when its CEOs lost focus

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Good morning. PepsiCo CEO Ramon Laguarta knows that he has a cola problem. He said “we’re not competing well in soft drinks” on his third-quarter earnings call. And last week KFC said it will switch to Coca-Cola products at its 3,700 U.S. restaurants, ending a 40-year partnership. As my colleague Phil Wahba writes, “Pepsi has suffered many setbacks in recent years, but perhaps few were as symbolic as being jilted by a long-time partner it used to own, KFC.” (Taco Bell and PepsiCo did renew their U.S. deal for 11 more years.) Why is PepsiCo’s beverage division struggling? I’d start with the decisions of Steve Reinemund, who ran PepsiCo from 2001 to 2006. Although he broadened the portfolio through the 2001 merger with Quaker Oats, which gave it Gatorade, he also created a less flexible distribution structure and added more complexity to the business. As PepsiCo leaders shifted to talking about crop yields and health trends, Coca-Cola moved in and struck a deal in 2003 to displace Pepsi at Subway, its largest fountain customer, business that Pepsi won back last year. His successor, Indra Nooyi, continued the trend, dividing the company’s portfolio into “Fun for You” (fatty, sugary foods), “Better for You” (lower-calorie or lower-fat versions), and “Good for You” (nutritious foods like oatmeal and hummus). In an era when cities were trying to ban big sugary drinks or impose a vice tax that put cola in the same camp as tobacco, attention to soda further waned. Meanwhile, Coca-Cola was shedding its low-margin bottling plants and focusing on building a “total beverage company” that resulted in 32 billion-dollar brands. PepsiCo has fewer billion-dollar brands but a lot more stuff, generating more than twice as much revenue as Coca-Cola with a market cap that’s about half as big. Are there advantages for PepsiCo in being a diversified conglomerate versus a pure-play beverage brand? No doubt, especially as trends like GLP-1 use increase demand for healthier foods. The brown fizzy drink that spawned these two competitors is a small part of the overall beverage market. And PepsiCo remains a powerhouse of innovation, marketing and talent. What’s slipped since the heyday of the cola wars is focus.Contact CEO Daily via Diane Brady at diane.brady@fortune.com United CEO has another idea: national service Scott Kirby caused a stir last week when he said the U.S. should have a national wealth tax, but the United Airlines CEO also offered another idea for national service. “I think if everyone had to spend a couple of years as a teacher, as a firefighter, serving in the military—I think that’d be good for our country.” How True Religion’s CEO is rebuilding the brand Michael Buckley is courting Gen Z and millennials with more affordable clothing, music partnerships, and in-store experiences, while letting go of something the brand once prided itself on: setting trends. “We like to be first to be second,” he said. The idea is to catch a trend once it’s proven rather than start it, and give customers what they want “at the price they want.” Thomas Miller, a prominent data scientist who’s a professor at Northwestern University, accurately predicted the 2024 presidential race and is using his methodology on the 2026 midterms. Since Oct. 14, the Dems’ odds of taking the majority have jumped from 71.5% to 77.6%. If the current odds in all those contests hold, the Democrats would win 18 of 35 races and shift the majority from 53-47 for the Republicans to 52-48 for the Democrats. Saudi Arabia wants to send 10 million barrels of oil a day around Hormuz. A former Treasury official says it may barely move prices
2026-10-23

Saudi Arabia wants to send 10 million barrels of oil a day around Hormuz. A former Treasury official says it may barely move prices

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
Saudi oil giant Aramco, officially the Saudi Arabian Oil Company, is racing to build a bigger back door around the Strait of Hormuz. Aramco CEO Amin Nasser said this week that the company is working to raise the capacity of its East-West Crude Oil Pipeline, also known as the Petroline, to 10 million barrels a day from about 7 million today, while running engineering studies on additional export routes. Nasser made the comments to Al Arabiya Business in his first interview since the war began. The plan echoes an announcement earlier this month from Saudi Energy Minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman, who told the WPC Energy Congress in Riyadh that the expansion would take two to three years. The goal is to reduce the kingdom’s reliance on the Strait of Hormuz, which has been largely choked off since the U.S. and Israel launched a joint military attack on Iran on Feb. 28. Saudi crude production fell below 7 million barrels a day for much of the war’s first six months, down from more than 10 million before the conflict, and recovered to about 8 million in September, according to the International Energy Agency. The Petroline spans Saudi Arabia, running roughly 750 miles from the Abqaiq processing hub near the Persian Gulf to the port of Yanbu on the Red Sea. Before the war, it typically carried around 2 million barrels a day. Aramco ramped it up within weeks, and the line hit its full 7 million-barrel capacity by late March, with about 2 million of those barrels feeding Saudi refineries rather than export markets. The pipeline helped Aramco post a 25% jump in first-quarter profit. Adding another 3 million barrels a day would be a major undertaking. But it may have little tangible impact on world fuel prices, according to Gilbert Metcalf, a visiting professor at the MIT Sloan School of Management and a former deputy assistant secretary for environment and energy at the U.S. Treasury Department. “If—and this is a big if—the Saudis can increase pipeline flow to 10 million barrels per day, it will put about 2% more oil into the global marketplace,” Metcalf tells Fortune. “That could lower prices a bit, but there’s a lot of uncertainty here. The pipeline is vulnerable to drone and missile attacks, as is much of the Saudi petroleum infrastructure. And it’s not entirely clear that there is available refinery capacity to process the oil.” That vulnerability isn’t hypothetical. The Petroline was first hit in May 2019, when Houthi drones struck a pumping station. This year it has been attacked repeatedly. An April 9 strike on a pumping station cut throughput by about 700,000 barrels a day before Saudi Arabia restored full capacity days later. On Sept. 10, drones struck the line in the Riyadh and Medina regions; Saudi officials said they were launched from Iraq. The attack forced a precautionary shutdown that lasted until Sept. 22, according to Kpler. And a Saudi energy-sector source told AFP that an Oct. 4 attack on a pumping station in Khurais halted flows again, though Bloomberg reported that oil continued to move. The Red Sea end of the route is under pressure, too. Yemen’s Iran-backed Houthis declared a maritime blockade of Saudi Arabia in July, have repeatedly attacked Saudi tankers, and in September seized control of the area around the Bab al-Mandab Strait, the southern gateway to the Red Sea. Southbound tankers leaving Yanbu must pass through it. Even if the pipeline runs smoothly, a bigger Petroline might do little to ease the world’s dependence on Hormuz. “I don’t think moving more oil to the pipeline takes much pressure off opening the Strait of Hormuz,” Metcalf says. “Despite what the U.S. Treasury and Energy Departments are saying, it does not appear that oil is flowing through Hormuz at anywhere near levels observed before the U.S. and Israeli attacks on Iran. Add to that threats in the Bab al-Mandab Strait and it’s clear that risks for Saudi oil production are as high now as they’ve ever been.” Tanker data supports his skepticism. Energy Secretary Chris Wright said this month that Gulf crude flows had recovered to between 80% and 90% of prewar levels. But Kpler figures show oil exports through Hormuz averaged just under 9 million barrels a day in September, roughly 44% of February’s level. Gulf producers are pursuing longer-term workarounds as well: the UAE is fast-tracking a new pipeline of its own. The Saudi government owns about 81.5% of Aramco. The kingdom’s sovereign wealth fund, the Public Investment Fund, holds 16%, and roughly 2.5% trades publicly. It is the world’s largest oil company and a major natural gas producer, with plans to raise gas output 80% by 2030 from 2021 levels. Yet even Aramco’s scale and an expanded pipeline may not be enough to shift the balance of power in the Middle East. “The irony is that the balance of regional power has tilted from Saudi Arabia towards Iran as a result of the attacks on Iran,” Metcalf says. “This would have been unthinkable just a year ago.” © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Looters hit 3,000 French businesses. Within hours, the haul was trending on Vinted under the riot’s date
2026-10-23

Looters hit 3,000 French businesses. Within hours, the haul was trending on Vinted under the riot’s date

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
As French protesters took to the street this week over the country’s escalating debt crisis, poor school conditions, and political gridlock, retailers were caught in the crossfire. More than 3,000 businesses were looted or damaged over the weekend, according to Commerce Minister Serge Papin, including shops in Paris’s Les Halles district. Those shops included Hermes, North Face, and Louis Vuitton. Just hours after looters rummaged through stores at the sites of demonstrations, online listings of cosmetic and skincare products and handbags began popping up on e-commerce platforms, raising concerns of widespread resale of stolen goods. By Monday evening, Papin and digital minister Anne Le Hénanff had summoned Vinted, French secondhand marketplace Leboncoin, Amazon, eBay, Meta, and TikTok to a videoconference to demand the listings come down. Vinted, Europe’s leading online secondhand marketplace, said it had taken steps to mitigate suspicious listings on its platform. “The sale of stolen goods, or soliciting them, is strictly prohibited on Vinted, and we have zero tolerance for it,” a Vinted spokesperson told Fortune in a statement. “We are actively collaborating with law enforcement and the French authorities, and we continually adapt our processes to detect and counter emerging risks and malicious behaviour.” “We’re seeing a clear spike in listings for luxury items,” a Vinted spokesperson previously told trade outlet RetailDetail, adding that such items don’t normally appear in those volumes. Retail theft has become a growing problem as organized retail crime becomes more sophisticated, ballooning from a $45 billion problem in 2024 to a projected $53 billion, according to security firm Pinkerton. Retailers report organized crime groups increasingly using a complex system of gift card fraud, phone scams, and supply chain theft, according to the National Retail Federation. The British Independent Retailers Association (Bira) said in 2024 that it estimates about 60% of items shoplifted from member companies are eventually sold online. The economic impact of business vandalism in France extends the economic consequences of retail theft. Rating agency Morningstar DBRS estimates insured losses from the unrest could reach €1.2 billion ($1.3 billion), assuming no further violence, and France’s main retail federation, the Fédération du commerce et de la distribution, puts direct damage at several hundred million euros. On Tuesday, French grocer Carrefour slashed its fiscal 2026 profit outlook, attributing the losses from riot-related damage. The announcement wiped out 15% of the company’s share price, equivalent to €1.7 billion ($1.9 billion) in market capitalization. “Around 50 of our stores were hit on day one, many were looted and the damage is considerable,” CEO Alexandre Bompard said. The company put the hit at €80 million to €200 million of operating income. Its shares fell as much as 15% on Wednesday, wiping out about €1.7 billion ($1.9 billion) in market value. At Monday’s meeting, the platforms committed to report suspicious listings to investigators; watch for abnormally low prices, bulk lots of new goods, and items still carrying anti-theft tags; warn buyers that buying stolen goods is also a crime; and publish how many listings they remove, with a first tally due within 48 hours, according to French tech site NeozOne. None of the e-commerce platforms responded to Fortune’s inquiry about whether there were stolen items listed on the respective sites. French President Emmanuel Macron gave a warning this week that thieves would be apprehended. In France, handling stolen goods is punishable by up to five years in prison and a €375,000 ($420,000) fine. “Those who looted will have to pay back, and those who sought to bring down the Republic will be held accountable,” he said. The French Senate begins debating a so-called “casseurs-payeurs” (“rioters pay”) bill on Oct. 27 that would hold everyone who took part in a gathering that caused damage collectively liable for it. Reselling stolen goods are appealing to retail criminals because many of the listed items are luxury cosmetics, fragrances, and electronics, which are small, easy to photograph, and in high demand. “Looted items are often sold quickly, in bulk and below market price, and the proceeds move through payment accounts that are themselves a fraud problem,” Serpil Hall, a strategic advisor at Datos Insights specializing in financial crime, told Fortune in an email. To e-commence platforms, however, the signs of stolen goods being resold on their marketplaces are clear. Items are often sold in bulk, priced below normal retail costs, described as being in great condition, and are often uploaded online within hours of a looting event. While finding hard proof of resold stolen goods is difficult, platforms can mitigate these listings by monitoring keywords and checking accounts, she said. Cybernews reported that the hashtag “October 17,” the date of many of the demonstrations, was trending on Vinted last week, but its use tapered off following increased reports of theft. Government and platforms have both worked to crack down on retail theft. The European Union requires marketplaces to collect and check trader details before they can trade, as well as to conduct random checks to assess if products were listed illegally. EU law also mandates marketplaces to tell buyers if it learns an illegally listed product was sold. According to TikTok, TikTok Shop does not allow consumer-to-consumer sales, and registered businesses must provide proof their products were legitimately purchased, including brand authorization or wholesaler certification. The platform has created a designated taskforce to conduct checks and remove suspicious listings and videos. An eBay spokesperson told Fortune the platform forbids the sale of stolen property. “We proactively use advanced technology, including algorithms and data-driven tools designed to identify suspicious patterns and potentially stolen merchandise, supported by dedicated teams that investigate and disrupt illicit activity,” the spokesperson said in a statement. “We also work closely with local, national and international law enforcement agencies, sharing information and supporting investigations to identify bad actors and prevent the sale of stolen goods.” Hall’s concerns go beyond just the resale of stolen goods, however. Revenue from illegal digital sales are often part of intricate money laundering schemes, she noted. Money from illegal sales moves through bank accounts, payment processors, and credit cards. Europol’s European Money Mule Action identified 10,759 “money mules,” or individuals who transfer money from accounts illegally, and 474 recruiters in 2023. The UK’s Financial Conduct Authority found in a September report that firms closed 238,396 suspected mule accounts in 2025, an increase from 233,269 the year before. The prevalence of money laundering schemes is an indication to Hall that more regulations are needed to address retail theft. “Put simply, the resale of stolen goods is often the final step of a payment fraud,” she said. Silicon Republic
How Ireland can become a leader in sovereign AI inferencing
2026-10-23

How Ireland can become a leader in sovereign AI inferencing

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
Dell’s Mark Hopkins explores why Ireland should stop focusing on every ‘layer of the AI stack’ and instead work towards becoming Europe’s home for sovereign AI inferencing. Despite a somewhat complex sounding name, sovereign AI inferencing is simply the process of running AI models on infrastructure that stays within a particular country or region and where it is subject to the places’ rules and regulations. It enables organisations, governments and countries to manage their own AI activity and to ensure that it meets certain standards, often in areas such as security, compliance, risk management and even sustainability. It can also give a business or country a competitive edge in a landscape that is increasingly AI-driven. With that in mind, many companies worldwide and indeed countries are putting more effort into developing their own technologies and infrastructure, both as a means of reducing external dependencies and maximising homegrown capabilities. For Mark Hopkins, a managing director at Dell Technologies Ireland, now is the time for Ireland to take a closer look at how it wants to compete both at home and abroad. He is of the opinion that, rather than focusing on every layer of the AI stack, the country’s real focus should be on becoming Europe’s home for sovereign AI inferencing. Hopkins said: “As Ireland holds the Presidency of the Council of the EU, it does so at a defining moment for Europe’s AI ambitions. The question facing Europe is no longer whether AI will transform economies and public services. It already is. “The challenge now is more practical; where AI will run, who will control it and whether organisations can deploy it at scale while maintaining trust, security and sovereignty. I believe that challenge presents Ireland with a significant opportunity.” He explained that many of Ireland’s geographical neighbours are striving to emerge as leaders in AI development, semiconductor manufacturing and research excellence, and that amid some of the noise, the country has the potential to find opportunities elsewhere. He said: “As we move from experimenting with AI to deploying it more broadly, inference, the process of putting AI models to work in the real world, could become Ireland’s defining contribution to Europe’s AI ecosystem. Ireland does not need to win the race to build every AI model. But it can become one of the places Europe trusts to run them.” Hopkins added: “Training models may capture headlines, but inference powers the everyday applications we all depend upon. It enables government departments to improve citizen services, hospitals to support clinical workflows, tax authorities to analyse compliance risks and businesses to make faster decisions. “Many European organisations will not build their own foundation models. Almost all, however, will need to deploy AI. The strategic question for Europe is not simply who trains AI, but who provides the trusted infrastructure that allows it to operate securely, responsibly and at scale.” This, he said, is where Ireland can lead. He explained that Europe’s ambitions are suitably aligned with Ireland’s strengths, which include world-class digital infrastructure, data expertise, skilled talent, public and private sectors working closely together and decades of experience in digital technology. Perhaps most importantly, he is of the opinion that AI sovereignty can address one of Europe’s most pressing challenges, the protection of highly sensitive materials. He said: “For years, digital sovereignty was largely a policy discussion. Today, it is an operational requirement. Public bodies and highly regulated industries increasingly need assurance that sensitive data remains protected, AI systems are transparent and workloads operate within trusted regulatory frameworks. “Inference is therefore becoming a strategic capability. It is where sovereignty moves from aspiration to execution. Organisations need confidence not only in AI models, but in the infrastructure, governance and safeguards surrounding them.” Hopkins said that as the holder of the EU Presidency, Ireland has a chance to drive the conversation on topics that affect both the country and the continent. He said: “The National Digital and AI Strategy provides a clear direction, while Ireland’s growing ecosystem of AI innovators is helping turn ambition into practical capability. Combined with the country’s international technology relationships and enterprise base, these foundations give Ireland a credible route to AI leadership.” But leadership will require focus and while “Ireland cannot be everything to everyone in the AI economy”, he explained, there is opportunity in playing to your strengths and owning a “critical part of the value chain”. “Inference may not attract the same attention as the race to build ever-larger models. Yet it is where AI becomes trusted, operational and economically valuable. It is where public services improve, businesses become more competitive and Europe’s sovereignty ambitions become real.” He added: “While Ireland holds the EU Presidency, it should champion the next phase of Europe’s AI agenda; turning ambition into trusted deployment at scale. Now Ireland must seize the opportunity. As Europe enters the deployment era of AI, inference can become Ireland’s national advantage within the European AI ecosystem.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Ireland risks leaving girls behind in its AI future, warns I Wish
2026-10-23

Ireland risks leaving girls behind in its AI future, warns I Wish

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
New research discovered that just more than one in four girls surveyed attending single-sex schools have access to computer science at a Leaving Cert level. New research from the 2026 I Wish Survey of Female Students’ Attitudes to STEM has shed light on how young girls risk being forgotten on the march towards Ireland’s AI future, if barriers to subject access continue. The 11th annual report gathered data from 662 primary school students and 1,660 female secondary school students, aiming to create a broader picture of how girls engage with and perceive the STEM space – as well as how they fit into it, both currently and with reference to the future. The research discovered that, in a landscape where the demand for digital specialists in Ireland is expected to increase by around 27pc in four years, just more than one in four girls surveyed attending single-sex schools have access to computer science at a Leaving Cert level. While a lack of subject availability creates a significant barrier to continuing education in STEM, it can also prevent girls from envisioning themselves in STEM roles in the future, blocking them from industries such as AI, cybersecurity, data analytics and cloud technologies. 60pc of contributors said a lack of confidence in STEM subject ability is a barrier to pursuing STEM careers; this figure has risen annually, from 55pc in 2025 and 50pc the year prior to that. Commenting on the report, the co-founder of I Wish, Caroline O’Driscoll, said, “We ask girls to be ambitious, but ambition needs assistance. A girl may discover an interest in technology, engineering or construction, only to find her school does not offer the technical and applied STEM subjects that would help her explore it. “Even when her school does offer these subjects, she may struggle to see a future for herself without encouragement, clear career information or women she can relate to in those roles. The growing confidence barrier we are seeing in our surveys is worrying and raises questions about whether girls are getting the support they need.” More than 60pc of contributors said that a lack of school visits by accomplished women in STEM roles can have a harmful impact. Two-thirds of contributors said the same of gender inequality and of insufficient career information. Always at the cutting edge of new innovation Join us to start Caring. Connecting. Growing together Taoiseach Micheál Martin, TD, who was present at the launch of the survey, said, “Ireland’s future competitiveness depends on developing the skills our economy needs. Strong growth in demand is projected over the coming four years for digital specialist jobs, yet women make up only one-fifth of this workforce. Addressing that imbalance is essential to building the talent our economy needs.” “I commend I Wish for giving girls a voice through its annual surveys and using those insights to shape its year-round programmes and inform the work of Government, schools and industry. “By identifying barriers and helping girls see what is possible, I Wish is creating opportunities for the next generation. We must work together to ensure girls have the access, encouragement and support to fulfil their potential.” I Wish is calling on those in the education space to make access to STEM subjects, strong career guidance and exposure to relatable role models more equitable. The organisation also highlighted the advantages of engaging in activities outside of STEM that can improve soft skills, such as sports, which often build confidence, teamwork and resilience. Social media was identified as a vehicle through which STEM careers can be made more visible. I Wish co-founder Gillian Keating said, “We cannot afford to carry today’s gender inequalities into tomorrow’s technologies. Girls must have the opportunity to shape STEM, not simply use it. Starting early, with practical experiences and female role models, helps girls discover their abilities and imagine where they could take them.” In February of next year, 4,000 secondary school girls from across Ireland will attend the 13th annual I Wish Festival at the RDS in Dublin to meet women working in STEM and explore potential career pathways. Greek parliament greenlights under-15 social media ban
2026-10-23

Greek parliament greenlights under-15 social media ban

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
The Hellenic Parliament in Athens. Image: © M.studio/Stock.adobe.com The new bill will come into force from January 2027. Greece’s parliament has voted to ban social media for children under 15, as countries across the world further attempt to shield children from the proven harms caused by unfettered access to the internet. The new bill, which will come into force from January 2027, sets the minimum age to access social media platforms at 15, and places the burden to reliably verify a user’s age on platform owners. Self-declared age is not considered enough under the new rules. The law assumes “substantial corporate responsibility”, said the country’s digital ministry in a translated press statement today (23 October), giving companies with knowledge of the algorithms behind their platforms the responsibility of implementing child safety measures. The upcoming Greek law is in line with the EU’s Digital Services Act, which regulates issues around online platform safety and transparency in the bloc. “Exclusive reliance on parental supervision creates inequalities. Every child is entitled to a common level of protection, regardless of the time, knowledge and capabilities of their family,” said Greek minister of state Akis Skertsos in the translated statement. Earlier this year, the country also introduced new rules to regulate AI use in schools. Greece, Ireland, Cyprus, Denmark, France, Italy and Spain are cooperating with the European Commission on a pan-European age verification solution, which promises to only transfer vital data – i.e. whether a user meets the age limit or not. Commission president Ursula von der Leyen, in her State of the Union speech in September, also announced the EU Kids Act, a legislative proposal that intends to ban social media for children under 13. A pan-EU law would also mean standardised access to social media across the bloc. Once the Greek law comes into force, it will be the first EU country to implement a children’s social media ban. In August, France’s top court blocked a proposed bill that would have banned social media for under-15s in the country from September, citing potential freedom-of-expression issues. Across the bloc, several other states – including Ireland, Spain and Germany – are mulling over restricting children’s access to social media after Australia became the first country to stop minors from using major platforms last year. In June, the UK said it would blanket-ban under-16s from a number of large user-to-user platforms such as Snapchat, TikTok, YouTube, Instagram, Facebook and X. Earlier this year, the EU greenlit a provisional deal to ban AI systems that generate non-consensual sexually explicit and intimate content or child sexual abuse material. FIT and Intel teaming up to advance AI skills among Ireland’s workforce

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
The partnership will initially focus on pilot programmes, before expanding to wider audiences and employer-led upskilling and reskilling initiatives. Fastrack into Information Technology (FIT) and Intel are partnering up on the new AI for Future Workforce Programme, which aims to boost AI skills among Ireland’s employees. The collaboration involves the development of an AI lab where learners can take part in the training. The partnership will initially focus on pilot programmes, before expanding to wider audiences and employer-led upskilling and reskilling initiatives. Feedback from learners, colleges and employers will be used to adapt and improve the process. The lab is FIT’s inaugural physical training space designed for one of its programmes, and will give participants access to the infrastructure and technology required to experiment with and apply AI in a practical setting. It will be equipped with computers, servers and the computing resources needed to support experimentation, testing, development and analysis, according to FIT. The programme is also Intel’s first Digital Readiness partnership in Ireland. The purpose of the initiative is to expand access to artificial-intelligence education, helping learners, educators and jobseekers develop the skills they need to succeed in an AI-driven economy. The programme will cover foundational and applied areas such as coding, computer vision, AI technologies, systems thinking, practical industry applications, and the responsible and ethical use of AI. Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. Initial opportunities identified by the partnership include programmes focused on AI for manufacturing, cybersecurity, sustainability and accessibility. The initiative intends to leverage FIT’s reach across Ireland’s further education and training sector and its relationships with further education colleges, education and training board centres, Youthreach centres and workforce upskilling initiatives. “FIT’s focus has always been on connecting people with the skills they need to participate fully in a changing labour market,” said Peter Davitt, the CEO of FIT. “As AI becomes relevant across a growing range of occupations and industries, it is essential that high-quality training is practical, accessible and closely aligned with the needs of employers. The creation of FIT’s first dedicated physical AI training space is an important step forward in how we deliver these skills.” He added: “Working with Intel will allow us to combine FIT’s national training network and industry-led approach with world-class learning resources, while the new AI lab will give learners the opportunity to explore the technology directly and apply their learning in a practical environment.” Earlier in the month, FIT announced plans to address the growing AI skills gap across Ireland by upskilling 400,000 people in AI by 2030. Mullingar’s Swaay raises €2m for scaling drinks cans printing tech
2026-10-23

Mullingar’s Swaay raises €2m for scaling drinks cans printing tech

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
From left: Mark O’Donnell, Jean-François Gaudreault, Sébastien Baril and Ciarán Gorman. Image: Swaay Technologies The funding round was led by BVP with participation from members of the BVP Investor Club. Irish industrial technology company Swaay Technologies has raised €2m in an equity investment round for its distributed manufacturing and printed-can platform for beverage brands. The start-up said it is building a full-stack platform to help beverage co-packers and producers adopt emerging direct-to-shape (DTS) printing technology, which can apply artwork directly onto drinks cans and remove the need for plastic labels and shrink sleeves. Swaay – founded this year – is based in Mullingar, Co Westmeath and has a production and technical hub in Trois-Rivières, Quebec, Canada. According to the company, it combines “short-run digital can production with contract fillers, distributors and ecosystem partners across North America”. Swaay said the new funding would take the company into commercial production through its DTS platform – which aims to embed new manufacturing innovation with software and data to deliver scalability – and support future roles in Mullingar. According to the company, its founders – CEO Ciarán Gorman, COO Sébastien Baril, CCO Mark O’Donnell and CTO Jean-François Gaudreault – have “decades of experience in the technology, beverage and print industries” between them. DTS printing eliminates the need for plastic labels and shrink sleeves by printing artwork directly onto a container, according to Swaay, potentially offering brands greater flexibility, easier recycling and shorter lead times. The company said this can help drinks producers navigate away from traditional considerations such as fragmented labelling rules, national deposit return schemes and demand for tailored messaging, which add complexity and result in “tens of billions of plastic shrink sleeves and labels” being used globally. “Brands care whether they can get a market-ready product in weeks, in the quantity they need and in the markets they want to serve or test,” said O’Donnell. “This round helps us serve this real demand commercially.” The company said that 1bn cans per year have moved to non-plastic labelling alternatives, and predicted that “billions more will follow as DTS closes the remaining gaps on cost, geographic availability and quality”. “I see a huge opportunity to build and scale Swaay into a global technology-driven company from Ireland,” said Gorman, who previously co-founded the Bevcraft Group. The funding round was led by BVP, an Irish investment firm headquartered in Dublin, with participation from members of the BVP Investor Club. BVP head of investments Stephen Burdock said: “Swaay has an operating team that has built and run manufacturing businesses before, and in this sector execution is everything. “Short-run labelling is a real and rising cost problem for brands, and cans keep taking share from other packaging formats. Swaay has a credible plan to make direct-to-shape printing work at scale.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. TUD graduate’s AI start-up Marc bags $5m to fund US expansion

TUD graduate’s AI start-up Marc bags $5m to fund US expansion

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-23

Visit Original Website
Aaron Devitt. Image: Anthony Anaya-Gorman/Marc The raise brings Marc’s total funding to $6m. New York-headquartered property management start-up Marc has raised $5m in an oversubscribed seed round to fund its US and Canadian expansion. The round was led by Work-Bench and MXV Capital with participation from Mark Ghermezian, the co-founder of customer engagement platform Braze; Fin co-founder Des Traynor; and Irish legal-tech Brightflag’s co-founder Ian Nolan, among others. The raise brings Marc’s total funding to $6m following its $1m raise in February this year, which was backed by 23 angel investors including Wayflyer’s Jack Pierse, Hostelworld’s Tom Kennedy and SoftCo’s Susan Spence. Founded by Technological University Dublin graduate Aaron Devitt, Marc’s platform uses AI to analyse fragmented sources of vendor contract and invoice data related to property units in order to help managers identify discrepancies that can lead to overpayment. For large institutional portfolios dealing with tens of thousands of invoices and lengthy agreements, checking every charge manually is effectively impossible, Marc argues. It estimates that every $1 unnecessarily lost through operating expenditure can erase as much as $20 in underlying asset value. Close to all of the invoices received across the property industry are currently unverified against their underlying contracts, it said. The funds will help the company hire across product, engineering and go-to-market over the next 12 months and reinforce its New York base as it scales to work with institutional real estate customers across North America, Marc said. The company said it works with customers across 30 US states and some of the largest operators in Canada. According to Marc, its customer base – whose combined assets under management are worth around $90bn – includes the likes of Magnolia Capital, Birge & Held and multiple multi-state SEC-regulated organisations. “We had already built a profitable business, but the scale of the opportunity we were seeing in North America made this the right time to accelerate,” said Devitt, the company’s CEO. “Institutional owners are facing the same challenges across increasingly large and complex portfolios, and demand for a better way to understand and control operating costs is growing. This investment allows us to build our team and product capabilities in the US and, importantly, be closer to our customers from our new base in New York.” Jonathan Lehr, co-founder and general partner at Work-Bench, added: “Institutional real estate is a huge market, but operating expenditure remains a major data blind spot for asset managers. “Marc is solving a clear and costly problem for institutional owners, and the traction it is already seeing across North America demonstrates the strength of that demand. Over time, the value of this data will compound as Marc builds a hyperlocal database of multifamily operating expenses spanning markets across the country.” Marc’s expansion, the company said, follows its “initial success in the UK and Ireland”, where its first property management product grew to more than 40,000 monthly logins.
