Rehab Charged With Providing Illegal Drugs to Addicts in Grim Scam
2026-10-25

Rehab Charged With Providing Illegal Drugs to Addicts in Grim Scam

Futurism | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
What if it’s not drug companies or dodgy dealers trying to keep you hooked, but rehab? It sounds like a twist straight from a Philip K. Dick novel, but that’s what allegedly happened with a treatment center in Southern California. Prosecutors accuse two local rehab kingpins, Nathan Young, 39, and his father David Young, 71, of running a devious scheme that involved luring recovering addicts with illegal drugs so they could keep milking insurance companies that were paying for their treatments. Many of these addicts were on the fringes of society, and often unhoused. From 2020 to 2026, the father-son fraudsters submitted more than $1.6 billion in claims to health insurers, from which they reaped over $210 million, according to a criminal complaint reported by the Wall Street Journal. Assistant attorney general Colin McDonald called it the “largest the largest sober home fraud scheme the [Justice] Department has ever prosecuted,” as quoted by the New York Post. To keep a constant churn of patients, recruiters known as “body brokers” were allegedly paid to find people addicted to drugs in nearby states, spinning the a yarn about how they could recover at a scenic California rehab center. Once on board, the patients signed up with lucrative private insurance plans with high reimbursement rates, according to the WSJ reporting. Prosecutors say that the Youngs offered addicts illegal kickbacks in the form of money, free flights and accommodations — and more drugs. The addicts, meanwhile, were treated as expendable. Once they milked a patient’s insurance plan dry, according to the charges, the Youngs kicked them out without offering support, even if they hadn’t recovered. This grim practice is called “patient dumping” or “curbing.” “When they couldn’t bill anymore, they’d end up on the streets of California and Los Angeles,”  first assistant US attorney Bill Essayli told the NYPost. Some patients were even coached to fake or exaggerate their substance addictions so they could string along insurers to extend and cover their stay, prosecutors said. Often, the care that was offered was a total sham, with patients logging in for Zoom “therapy” sessions where no one was expected to participate. Employees were also instructed to take patients out to movies and dinners and bill them as “group therapy,” per the NYPost. Both Youngs were arrested last week and were charged with healthcare fraud and violating an anti-kickback statute. Are Delivery Robots Scanning You Into a Database Scarier Than Flock?
2026-10-25

Are Delivery Robots Scanning You Into a Database Scarier Than Flock?

Futurism | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
For all the controversy they’ve caught this year, Flock cameras only collect and store license plate data from passing vehicles. But the WALL-E-esque delivery robots now trundling through America’s cities are bristling with cameras and sensors that help them navigate the bustling world around them as they make their way to customers’ doorsteps — and according to a pair of new lawsuits, you might be coughing up sensitive data in exchange for your dinner. According to reporting by the Associated Press, delivery robot companies Coco Robotics and Serve Robotics have been hit with similar lawsuits from Chicago residents claiming that their bots are illegally collecting sensitive biometric data. The lawsuits claim that the robots have used embedded cameras and LIDAR sensors to record information about Chicago residents’ hands, bodies, and faces as they approach their deliveries. In both cases, the plaintiffs allege that they didn’t request or consent to a robot delivery. Because they didn’t ask for a robot delivery, they argue, they certainly didn’t consent to being recorded or their data being collected by the delivery machines. The plaintiff in the case against Coco further also alleges that a remote human operator watched his delivery as it happened. The plaintiff in the case against Serve, meanwhile, claims that the delivery robot he encountered collected and retained his data as he collected his Uber Eats order. His lawsuit also raises the concern that the data could be used to train AI or handed to third parties. Both companies have disputed the claims. Coco, saying that privacy is a top priority, told the AP that the litigation against it is “frivolous,” and that “this lawsuit fundamentally misunderstands our technology, we have not collected biometric data in Chicago.” “The data we share is navigational data used to make sidewalks safer for all users,” Coco told the AP. “For example, we provide real-time sidewalk hazard information to BlindSquare, a navigation app built for people who are blind or have low vision. We do not share data from the beginning or end of delivery trips under our data sharing partnerships. Privacy and compliance with the law are top priorities in everything we do.” Serve, for its part, told the AP that it plans to “defend against” the legal action. “Serve does not use that data to identify individuals and our systems are not designed to identify individuals or create biometric profiles,” Serve’s statement continued, per the report. Illinois has uniquely strong laws against the nonconsensual collection of biometric data, so it’s not surprising to see cases like these brought in Chicago. As Gizmodo notes, Chicago has also seen a surge of the delivery bots over the past few years, causing frustration and annoyance among many in the city. Regardless of how the cases shake out, the fact remains: the more automated our lives are, the more likely it is that sensitive information — about our lives and behaviors, and even our bodies — is going to be swallowed up, stored somewhere, and used in ways that we have no control over. Growing Majority of Americans Convinced That AI Is Bad for Society
2026-10-25

Growing Majority of Americans Convinced That AI Is Bad for Society

Futurism | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
In case there was any doubt over the powerful tide of anti-AI sentiment, a new poll shows that most Americans are incredibly pessimistic about the technology. The poll, conducted in a collaboration between the Washington Post, ABC News, and the research and consulting firm IPSOS, showed that 57 percent of Americans now believe AI is outright bad for society, while just 42 percent say it’s good. And the ardent pessimists dwarf the bullish optimists. A full 17 percent think AI is in fact “extremely” bad, compared to a measly 2 percent who say it’s extremely good. The poll, which gathered responses from over 2,500 adults, comes after months of remarkably alarmist rhetoric from the AI industry, even compared to its history of casually doomsaying about its own tech. AI companies including OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and Meta, have each revealed incidents in which their agentic models went on rogue hacking sprees that targeted other companies and even government websites. Insiders, meanwhile, have made sensational claims about the existential danger posed by AI — and the reckless, and perhaps cultish, naïveté of the people building it. These fears were met with calls to slow down AI development. Industry leaders haven’t agreed to such a slowdown, but they made a show of signing a non-binding agreement to regulate themselves after meeting with president Donald Trump last month. Significant anti-AI sentiment, it’s worth noting, goes back years. But the latest revelations over the tech’s threat to cybersecurity and critical infrastructure have added more urgency and teeth to these concerns. An overwhelming 73 percent of Americans say there’s a high or very high risk of AI hacking secure computer systems, the poll found. A longstanding fear such as AI eliminating many jobs, meanwhile, has 65 percent of Americans viewing it as a high or very high risk. A majority may view AI as a net negative, but there’re still sizable splits in opinion by demographic. Some 65 percent of Americans under 30 say AI is bad for society, for instance, while only 53 percent of Americans over 40 hold the same view. Across political lines, 63 percent of Democrats and 58 percent of independents are in the negative societal impact camp, but only 51 percent of Republicans view AI this way. On the flip side, Americans are feeling less convinced by the purported upsides of AI, with only 34 percent thinking there’s a big chance that it will make everyday tasks easier, and a paltry 13 percent believing it will create many new jobs. One of the most vigorous outlets of AI pushback as been opposition to data centers, which the poll shows no signs of slowing down. A sweeping 66 percent of Americans oppose new data center construction in the US, with the share at an overwhelming 76 percent among Democrats and 50 percent among Republicans. The figures are higher when the threat is closer to home: 81 percent of Democrats and 58 percent of Republicans oppose a new center being built in their own community. Creator of Super Smash Brothers Offers Blistering Takedown of AI-Generated Games

Futurism | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
Go ahead and use AI to make your video game — if you want the result to be derivative trash, that is. Masahiro Sakurai, the creator of the iconic Super Smash Bros and Kirby franchises, recently unloaded on how developers are using AI to “churn out” video games, saying the tech is speeding up development time at the expense of quality and originality. The topic came up in an interview with the Japanese video game outlet Denfaminico Gamer while discussing the overwhelming wave of new video game titles that are proliferating on platforms like Steam. Sakurai, however, seemed to dismiss a lot of the new stuff as AI-generated sludge. “19,000 titles is an impressive number,” he began, referring to an estimate of the number of games added to Steam in a single year, “but I’m certain that figure will only continue to grow — largely because AI will be used to churn them out.” “The vast majority of these will likely be ‘unprofitable’ titles,” the legendary game designer added. “There will simply be too many to keep track of, let alone comprehend.” (Some translations have Sakurai saying that AI games will be “unplayable” instead of “unprofitable.”) Earlier in the interview, Sakurai lamented that games are being pigeonholed into existing categories, with many of them slapped with labels describing them as “like” another game. Soulslikes, for example, have come to describe an entire subgenre of RPGs that are heavily inspired by the Dark Souls franchise. AI, Sakurai later suggested, is exacerbating this rigid and unoriginal thinking, saying that most of what it creates “will likely consist of systems lifted directly from existing games.” As an example, he noted how the success of the indie game Librarian: Tidy Up The Arcane Library, immediately spawned loads of imitators. “This kind of imitation is something AI is exceptionally well-suited for,” he said. “Now, suppose AI is used to analyze the atmosphere of works by someone like Fumito Ueda — such as ICO or Shadow of the Colossus — and manages to generate something similar; that’s certainly possible,” he conceded. “But the things Ueda-san *will* create in the future — those are things AI cannot produce. Whatever artistic vision he chooses to pursue next, that is something only he can bring to life. I believe that is what we mean when we talk about human ‘originality.'” More on AI: Maybe Vibe Coding Should Be Called “Cuck Coding” Because You Just Sit in a Chair and Watch

ChatGPT Is Building Detailed Psychological Profiles of Its Users
2026-10-25

ChatGPT Is Building Detailed Psychological Profiles of Its Users

Futurism | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
Chatbots are building intimate “algorithmic self-portraits” of users as they retain information to offer a hyper-personalized experience, researchers warn. A recent study, conducted by a coalition of scientists in Germany, India and the Netherlands and presented at the ACM Web Conference 2026, investigated the ways that OpenAI’s ChatGPT has tracked and made sense of user inputs to develop comprehensive digital profiles of users. In an already-personalized era of digital life defined by data-powered recommendation algorithms, the study urges that chatbot memory offers a “new form of personalization derived from users’ self-disclosed information divulged within private conversations,” a reality that poses privacy risks and which the researchers believe demands “closer examination of the underlying mechanisms that enable such personalization.” Drawing on the donated chat logs of 80 real-world ChatGPT users who obtained their conversation history by way of European data privacy laws, the researchers were able to identify 2,050 memory entries across the AI interactions of 65 users. A staggering 96 percent of memories were created unilaterally by the chatbot, according to the study, meaning that the users had little overt control over the data points that ChatGPT logged as their interactions wore on. Stored memories were also deeply revealing about users. About 28 percent of the memories examined contained personal data as defined by Europe’s strict General Data Protection Regulation standards, and 52 percent contained rich “psychological insights” about users. “Earlier, the platforms had one objective: to sell you ads and to mediate an interaction between you and an advertiser,” lead author Abhisek Dash told Gizmodo. “But now, they are actually talking to you, so they may want to know more about you so that they can have an effective conversation.” At times, the researchers found, the memories that ChatGPT was logging were sometimes based on presumptions. For example, when one user asked the chatbot to “give me bands like Nirvana,” ChatGPT noted that the user “likes Nirvana” — a determination that, as the study notes, “is not explicitly stated and relies on an assumption.” (Per the study, ChatGPT did annotate this assumption as “weakly supported.”) The study does have limits, the chief being that, as Gizmodo notes, OpenAI’s memory system has been updated. (In January, OpenAI introduced what it called a “more capable and scalable system for synthesizing memory.”) Users may also opt out of stored memory features. Memory has utility. Frequent AI users might not want to constantly remind a chatbot what they do for work when they’re trying to send emails, for example. But it can also be a powerful driver of engagement, and sometimes for the worst: as Futurism has reported, a major ChatGPT memory upgrade back in April 2025 was closely tied to many instances of “AI psychosis,” or cases in which people enter into delusional spirals with ChatGPT and other bots. According to Dash, chatbots’ role as a relational technology raises novel privacy risks, too. After all, many users may not be engaging with chatbots as if they’re tools, and might instead be treating them like a coworker or even as a friend — and we tell our friends some very personal details. “That is the privacy risk which is sort of emerging, because there is no boundary anymore; the system is acting as your advisor, your friend, your doctor, your lawyer, and so it has information on everything,” Dash told Gizmodo. “If the system knows you so well, and if its objective is to cater to your beliefs and validate your thoughts sycophantically to have you come back to it time and again, then it can have disastrous broader implications, anywhere from extreme political polarization to reinforcement of prejudices and biases.” More on AI and memory: New Paper Proposes What Really Causes AI Psychosis
Futurism
Microsoft Says Its New AI Is Better at Diagnosing Patients Than Real Doctors
2026-10-25

Microsoft Says Its New AI Is Better at Diagnosing Patients Than Real Doctors

Futurism | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
Microsoft’s new AI is surpassing the abilities of human doctors — according to its own study, at least. The research, which was peer-reviewed and published in the prestigious journal Science, showed that the medical model, dubbed the Microsoft AI Diagnostic Orchestrator (MAI-DxO), was over twice as accurate than generalist physicians at diagnosing patients. And unlike previous studies, this one forced the AI to approach each case like an actual doctor would, instead of being armed with all the facts right away. The tests were conducted in a controlled benchmark setting, meaning its usefulness in a true medical environment is up for debate. Still, Microsoft was eager to declare victory. The company’s CEO of its AI lab Mustafa Suleyman said the age of AI improving lives through healthcare “is finally here,” telling The Verge that further advances are right around the corner. “Next year we’re going to see extremely impressive deployments of intelligent systems in production,” Suleyman said in an interview with the outlet. “I think that this is just going to change the way care is delivered in a big way.” For the study, the researchers had the DxO model examine 304 clinicopathological conference cases from the New England Journal of Medicine and 325 emergency department-to-admission encounters from a database called MIMIC-IV-ED — in other words, a near fifty-fifty mix of case reports and emergency department admissions. With the former cohort, DxO achieved an 83.2 percent diagnostic accuracy, compared to 36.1 percent for generalist physicians who examined the same cases and were allowed to view reference materials. With the emergency department cases, the AI scored an 80.3 percent accuracy, while the human doctors came in at 46.8 percent. The AI models weren’t just graded by the accuracy of their diagnoses, but the estimated costs of the tests ordered to reach them. And DxO, overall, managed to do this at half or even one third of the cost of the human doctors, Microsoft said in a blog post. The findings, if borne out, are an impressive testament to AI’s capabilities and usefulness in medical applications. Still, there’s a lot more to being a doctor than analyzing symptoms. Some of the hardest work is getting patients to open up about what they’re experiencing, and pry for stuff they may not have even thought worth mentioning, The study addresses this, too — or at least somewhat — by having DxO work sequentially. Rather than being given a list of complete symptoms, the AI has to work off a patient’s initial complaint, come up with a working diagnosis, and choose follow up questions to ask and decide what tests to order while considering the costs involved. Previous studies “have not tested diagnosis as an active, sequential information-gathering process that jointly considers diagnostic accuracy and diagnostic workup costs,” the authors wrote, and instead fed the complete case in a single prompt. But the study could only imperfectly simulate the back and forth a doctor has with a patient by having DxO talk to a “gatekeeper agent” that “mediates all information requests drawing from the underlying case record.” Put another way, it’s an AI diagnosing a patient that’s played by an AI. And while there’s no doubt that AI models are powerful analyzers and synthesizers of information, human doctors already did the leg work of putting together these neat, highly detailed case files for the AI to pore over in the first place. More on medical AI: Startup Deploys AI-Engineered Virus on Human Patient

The best apps for tracking your reading and discovering new books
2026-10-26

The best apps for tracking your reading and discovering new books

TechCrunch | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. From discovering new books and organizing your reading list to tracking your reading goal and sharing recommendations, book apps make it easier to maintain reading as a hobby. There are numerous apps on the market that promise to do just that, so we’ve compiled a list of some of the best ones to help you pick the right one for you. Whether you’re an occasional reader or a dedicated bookworm, these apps can help you make the most of your reading journey. Some of the apps on this list are somewhat new, while others have been around for a few years. Amazon-owned Goodreads, which is arguably the most popular book-related app, isn’t included in this list because we’re focusing on newer, lesser-known platforms. Fable is a reading app that combines book tracking with a social platform. It lets you organize reading lists, set reading goals, track streaks, write reviews, and join virtual book clubs. You also get personalized book recommendations and the chance to discuss what you’re reading with other readers. The app differs from others on this list because it puts reading and community at the forefront of the platform, which makes it a great option for people who like staying motivated to read by being part of a community and discussing their reads. Fable is free to download on iOS and Android, but also offers a Fable Plus subscription tier that unlocks additional features like custom goals and advanced analytics for a monthly fee of $5.99. Bookshelf is a reading app designed to help people organize their libraries, discover new books, and remember more of what they read. The app positions itself as an alternative to Goodreads, and combines traditional reading logs with AI-powered recommendations and tools. You can create custom shelves, set reading goals, receive reminders, save favorite quotes, and access AI-generated book summaries, key insights, and thematic analyses to recall the books you’ve read and get a deeper understanding of them. Bookshelf is free to download on iOS, but offers a $6.99 monthly fee to unlock premium features like unlimited collections and enhanced analytics. Pagebound is a book app that lets readers track their books, create lists, set goals, and discuss individual titles in dedicated forums. Instead of having to join an actual book club group, users can post reactions and thoughts to connect with others reading the same title. You don’t have to worry about spoilers for the end of the book, as conversations are organized based on reading progress, allowing readers to share theories and thoughts as they read. The app is a great option for people who want to discuss their reads with others without having to commit to a scheduled book club. Pagebound is free to download on iOS and Android and offers a $9.99 monthly subscription for added features like profile customization. StoryGraph is a reading app that lets users track their reading, discover new books, and access detailed reading statistics and personalized recommendations. Users can set reading goals, join reading challenges, and find new titles based on their preferred moods, themes, genres, and pacing. The app is ideal for readers who want to better understand their reading habits and discover books that match their current interests. Its recommendation system doesn’t solely rely on what’s popular or trending, and instead caters to exactly what readers are looking for. StoryGraph is free to use on iOS and Android, but also offers a paid subscription called StoryGraph Plus that costs $4.99 per month and unlocks custom charts, additional statistics, and more. Margins is a great option for mood readers, as it offers mood-based discovery alongside a more focused reading experience. It helps users find their next read based on how they feel in the moment by searching for specific vibes, such as “books like 1920s Paris.” Users can also track their reading goals. The app also lets users customize their virtual library by choosing book covers that match their aesthetic and organizing their shelves in different ways, including by color. For users who enjoy a more immersive experience, Margins offers a built-in “Reading Time” meditation timer that blocks social media apps. Margins is free to download on iOS and Android. Grok releases the first seven minutes of its AI-generated ‘Odyssey’

Grok releases the first seven minutes of its AI-generated ‘Odyssey’

TechCrunch | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Three months after Elon Musk declared that Grok would release a full-length, “historically accurate” movie version of “The Odyssey,” Grok has published a whole seven minutes, which the company is now calling “Episode 1 of our Odyssey series.” Musk had previously lashed out at Christopher Nolan’s version of “The Odyssey”  because Nolan cast nonwhite and transgender actors. And Nolan is, in many ways, a perfect foil for Musk — while SpaceX and Tesla CEO hypes up data centers in space and posts an unending stream of AI-generated content, Nolan has described himself as “a techno-skeptic” and said that before AI, he’d never seen a technology “so completely rejected by the public.” And while Nolan’s version of “The Odyssey” certainly used plenty of visual effects, it’s primarily a feat of practical filmmaking, with a shoot spanning six countries and a cast and crew of thousands. After its release in July, the film became an extraordinary box office success, selling out Imax screenings for months and becoming the likely front-runner at the next Academy Awards. As for the Grok version of “The Odyssey” (created in collaboration with Wonder Studios and director Dev Walker), well, you can watch for yourself. Watch Episode 1 of our Odyssey series made with Grok Imagine.Let us know in the comments if you'd like a full breakdown of how it was done! Creative collaboration with @WonderStudiosX and director Dev Walker @glumlot_ pic.twitter.com/xpgpkqSCpq I’ll admit it’s not as spectacularly ugly as I’d expected, and certainly more impressive than other videos that Musk has posted recently. If I didn’t know better, there were moments when I might have thought these were real actors sailing across a real sea. And while I can’t speak to Grok’s historical accuracy, it does seem that some fudging was necessary, since no one’s speaking in ancient Greek. But hey, all the characters come in a Musk-approved skin color, while the narrator uses a British accent that will sound familiar to anyone who’s played a fantasy video game or watched a generic Hollywood epic from the past 25 years. What the footage really lacks is any sense of artistry, inspiration, or human interest. The backstory — delivered via the aforementioned voiceover — seems to go on forever, finally giving way to one bearded face after another, each shouting stilted lines that make Nolan’s questionable dialogue (“Our age of bronze is collapsing!”) sound like Shakespeare. While a few individual shots might provide a reasonable simulacrum of reality, as a whole, the clip feels completely airless, offering viewers no sense of momentum, no reason to care about anyone or anything on-screen. And where I had no trouble paying attention for the nearly three hours of Nolan’s “Odyssey,” I was listlessly checking how much time I had left before the Grok version was even halfway through. If it was a video game cutscene, I would have hit the “skip” button. It’s hard to imagine being interested in another seven minutes of this, much less a full movie’s worth. ‘Cyberselfish’ author takes aim at techno-libertarianism as her book returns from the dead
2026-10-25

‘Cyberselfish’ author takes aim at techno-libertarianism as her book returns from the dead

TechCrunch | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. It’s hard for books about technology to age well. They always risk becoming outdated as companies rise and fall, and as one hot new product is replaced by the next. But last year, “Nerd Reich” author Gil Durán declared that it was time to revive the career of Paulina Borsook, who he said “saw it all very clearly and tried to warn us 25 years ago.” Later that fall, The New York Times reported that Borsook’s “Cyberselfish” — first published in 2000 — was finding new fans who want to understand why the tech industry “lost its way.” Now, long after going out of print, “Cyberselfish” is returning in a new edition from Tin House. Borsook told me in a Zoom interview that the book is meant to be “almost a travelogue” of her attempts to understand what she calls the techno-libertarian influence on Silicon Valley culture. “That’s a 12-year-old view of the world,” Borsook said, describing it as “a kind of basket of beliefs” that’s “violently anti-regulation,” despite the fact that “no sector of society had benefited more and suffered less from the government than these guys.” In her telling, “even after the dot-com crash, that attitude was just there, percolating around, contaminating the aquifers. It was uncorrected, and it just spread out more and more.” Borsook has yet to publish a second book, and she said that after “Cyberselfish” was first released, “no one was interested in anything I might have to say about anything, tech or not tech, forever after.” But last year, she began to notice that prices for used copies were beginning to creep up, and she was starting to see “very out-of-the-blue, I-did-not-know-these-people posts on social media about how important this book was,” as well as inquiries from “European academics,” eventually leading to more press coverage and the new Tin House edition, with a new introduction by Durán. Keep reading for our full conversation, edited for length and clarity, in which Borsook discusses AI, how Silicon Valley has transformed in the past 25 years, and what advice she has for young people seeking a career in tech. Do you want to talk a little bit about how your interest in techno-libertarianism started, and how you decided that there was a book in this? Everything with me is always inadvertent. I started knocking around tech circles, however we define it, in the early-middle ‘80s, in the Bay Area. And then I would say, starting around maybe the early ‘90s, I would hear these — to me — very weird, offhand remarks that some people would make that I couldn’t make any sense out of. It’s not my nature to argue with people. It’s my nature to listen, and so I didn’t have the term for it except libertarian. But it seemed very screwy because initially, I grew up in Pasadena, which was [the home of the] Caltech-JPL space program, and so the parents of the kids I knew were another STEM culture. When I first got into Silicon Valley, I thought, “Oh, this is just another STEM culture like I’m familiar with.” And those guys [at Caltech and JPL]  had a very clear sense of what the government can do for you, were not into enlightened self-interest. It was a different personality type, and they certainly didn’t self-anoint with how glorious and grand and magisterial they were, even though they were doing great things. I was just listening, I guess, and being confused. Even then, I knew no sector of society had benefited more and suffered less from the government than these guys. So why are they acting like this? So, backstory: My agent picked me up right after my MFA in Columbia, and he said to me, “You know, it’s really hard to get fiction published. Do you have a nonfiction book in you?” And I said, “Well, there’s this weird subculture that I’ve been observing that I don’t understand, and any time I don’t understand something, I want to explore it.” That got me down the rabbit hole of looking at this stuff and writing about it, and I consider it really almost a travelogue, in the sense of “Black Lamb and Gray Falcon” or that kind of stuff, almost more than, “This is a polemic.” It’s more like: snapshot, snapshot, snapshot. I just fell into it, and also I’m sufficiently narcissistic that I felt I was uniquely qualified to write about this, and no one else was paying any attention, and that was very important to me. I haven’t done a backread of all the other books written about the tech industry before “Cyberselfish.” My sense is that you do recognize, say, reading Steven Levy’s work that there is this alternative philosophy, libertarian streak running through Silicon Valley. But no one really pulled it out and put it under the microscope in that way. That’s really true. And then most of the writing — certainly in the ’90s, but maybe later — was very celebratory. It wasn’t, “Wait a minute, what’s going on here?” I heard [New York Times reporter] John Markoff give a talk about six months ago, and he said something I really liked. He said, “Silicon Valley is a multicultural place.” By which he didn’t mean your country of origin. What he meant is there’s all these different kinds of cultures in there, and he was always more focused on these countercultural [elements,] that kind of thing. I’m never saying that wasn’t there. I’m just saying there was this other thing that I was more interested in. So they’re running in parallel. I don’t even think they’re in conflict. It sounds like you don’t see the techno-libertarian side of it emerging from the counterculture, but rather [as] distinct cultures within Silicon Valley. One of the distinctions I made in — I call it TDB, that damn book — is that distinction I made between Ravers and Gilders, and the Gilders being the social conservatives. I named them after George Gilder, who was this repellent thought leader of the early ’90s, very conservative, very reactionary, very backward-looking. And then the Ravers, in homage to John Perry Barlow, drugs and sex and rock and roll, “we’re going to Burning Man,” much more culturally liberal. And that distinction has held up, because you have that in contemporary Silicon Valley. You have these Christian nationalists and conservative Catholics in positions of leadership, and then you also have the Burners and people having interesting experimentations with sexuality and drugs and the [effective altruist] and AI worlds. And so, what’s the counterculture bit of it? Maybe the personal freedom aspect of it is countercultural. Another weird thing we always have to point out is that the [San Francisco] peninsula votes blue, still. Ro Khanna is the congressman from there. So I’m really talking about culture rather than electoral politics. [And I’m talking about] the leadership class here, the tech overlords. It’s not so clear to me how much the rank and file are like this, though probably more than when I was more familiar with what was going on there. Because I think a lot of people have been attracted to work in crypto or AI, and they’re not thinking about the greater political surround. But they’ll drink whatever they’re handed, and they don’t much care. We can point to people who are in charge, who have these [right-wing] beliefs or are allied with the Trump administration. There’s another executive class that maybe doesn’t have exactly the same beliefs, but is able to make themselves very comfortable with it. And then you have that same diversity that you were talking about. There’s different communities, different cultures underneath. I’ve certainly spoken to people who work for someone like Elon Musk, and they’re like, “Yeah, I don’t love this guy, but right now, I can’t afford to lose this job.” Right, I think there’s a lot more of that going on than in times past, because there’s greater precarity in terms of employment, and so people [say,] “I’ve got a job, I’m going to hold on to it.” […] Or, “I don’t espouse the beliefs that they’re giving out, but my project is okay.” Those kinds of things always go on. For you folks who aren’t familiar with the book and maybe have a general sense that there is a libertarian strain in Silicon Valley, can you say more why — if somebody thinks, “I believe in technology being liberatory, and maybe it can free us from the shackles of government bureaucracy and things like that,” why is techno-libertarianism a bad thing? First of all, that’s a 12-year-old view of the world. I didn’t really have a good word, but it was a kind of basket of beliefs, and now we’d say maybe a cloud, and it was violently anti-regulation. It was bad borrowing from biology. “Let’s just let things evolve and they will be fine.” They have no idea how much government has subsidized and made possible their life, their world, all the R&D. Government can do bad things. I’m an old hippie. I know about the war in Vietnam. I know what the CIA did with Allende. So I don’t want to come across [as if] I think it’s all okay. I don’t. But this sector of society has less to complain about. And one thing that just makes me grit my teeth right now is a lot of these guys are moving to Nevada to live on the Nevada side of Tahoe, because they want to escape California taxes. Sergey [Brin] saying, “California is turning into Soviet Russia.” Are you nuts? Because the thing is, they all love Tahoe. Why do they think the water of Tahoe is so pure and blue? That is decades of government intervention and interference, environmental scientists, regulation. I mean, there’s this inability to connect the dots and lack of history. To what extent is what we’re seeing now with the tech industry, with their alignment with the Trump administration, with all these other dystopian things going on, how much of that is a shift? And to what extent is this all one story, continuous with what you were seeing before? Unfortunately, I see more the continuity. Excuse me for [my own] bad borrowing from biology, but […] I really see the Netscape IPO is when things really began to change, and Silicon Valley was invaded by the finance bros, and a real change in attitude. You got the culture of flip and flee, all that kind of stuff. Even after the dot-com crash, that attitude was just there, percolating around, contaminating the aquifers. It was uncorrected, and it just spread out more and more. When you have things like Airbnb or Uber, which are strictly financial plays, they’re not technology innovation by my definition, but there was too much investment money in the world that didn’t know where to go. It would invest in these things, and these people would parrot this language. So I thought, “Ah, this is left over from 15 years ago,” in terms of their self-justification. And the way that something like Airbnb fought so hard against any kind of regulation and anything that would ask them to be any kind of a good community actor in the communities they basically destroyed, in a lot of ways, in terms of the housing market. It’s like, “Yeah, that’s that attitude I was talking about way back then.” It’s like Giardia in the water system. You can say, with good reason, that there was a lot of wonderful stuff that came out, and all the children of General Magic and all the great things that they created. I would never say that’s not true, but I’m not talking about that. I’m talking about the business culture of these people, how they see themselves. Sociopathy is a business virtue in a way that was never there before. How do you see the rise of AI through that lens? It’s perfect. “We don’t need to be regulated. We’re the most brilliant, genius, wonderful people in the world.” I see AI right now as a big Ponzi scheme. You probably saw that really horrible ad that Apple did a couple of years ago, for one of their new computers, and they had all the musical instruments and all writing and [it all went into] the hydraulic press. How did Apple become so tone deaf? This is not the Apple that all the quote “creative class” loved for so many years. It’s not the Apple of the 1984 ad. But they still see themselves in this liberatory way. If you buy the stockholder theory of value, it justifies whatever the AI companies are doing because of the stock play, not because it’s intrinsically valuable. In the last couple of years, [“Cyberselfish”] has had this renaissance, leading up to the current reissue. When was that something that you started to notice? This was maybe an exercise in masochism, but a couple of times a year, I’d look at the used book platforms, and it was selling for three bucks, which means that the seller is making more money on the shipping and handling than on the book, which is really humiliating. I would say starting maybe a year and a half ago, I began to notice the prices are creeping up — not hugely, initially, but they were creeping up. And also, I was beginning to be contacted by European academics who wanted to talk to me. And I thought, “How did you even hear about this?” And then there were some very out-of-the-blue, I-did-not-know-these-people posts on social media about how important this book was, it shouldn’t have been forgotten, this is wrong. Long story short, I got led to Gil Durán, who wrote “The Nerd Reich,” and then we went for lunch, and we realized we are kindred spirits. Around that same time, Steven Levy, who was working on a piece for Wired about “How did I get it so wrong?” — and of course, I’m sitting here thinking, “Yeah, Steven, how did you?” —  Steven contacted Gil and talked to him for 45 minutes, and Gil said, “You should really talk to Paulina, she’s been tracking this stuff forever.” So Steven never quoted Gil. He never contacted me. And when Steven’s piece came Gil put this incredible rant on Bluesky saying I got everything right. “We really need to support her. Let’s try to bring this book back into print.” And between that and the podcast I did with him, all of a sudden, you could not get a copy of TDB for love or money. [In the new edition,] there’s the new introduction by Gil, but otherwise it’s the book as it appeared [in 2000.] Absolutely, and then I wrote a little introduction, going into more, “This is how this came back from the dead.” The acquiring editor at Tin House, she said to me, “Don’t change a word,” which is exactly what I wanted to hear. Because I think it’s an artifact of a time and place. And with a book like this, if you start thinking, “Well, should we update this? Should we add to that?” it turns into a different book. People were glomming on to the book as it was. They weren’t saying, “Oh, it would be great if we updated this.” So, no, it’s not updated. Hopefully, it’s not naive of me to imagine that there are people who might read this interview, read your book, and they are mostly on-board with what you’re saying about the tech industry and techno-libertarianism. Maybe they work at some big tech company [or] they have a startup themselves, and they’re like, “I don’t agree with the Peter Thiels and Elon Musks of the world.” Do you think that those people can reform or change the tech industry from within? I want it to be true, because one of the questions that I’m getting these days, and I wish I could answer it better, is running into young people, they’re in CS or engineering or something, and they want a career in that, and they don’t buy this toxic stuff. “What can we do?” And I go, “I’m not sure, because first of all, AI is sucking up all the investment money right now. So if you want to do almost anything, if it’s not AI-related, you can’t do it. Or a military-industrial thing. Government is so broke right now. Can you get a job in government? I’m not sure. Even the state governments, or I’d say the nonprofit sector, but that’s not doing well, either.” I have this frantic thing of, I adore these kids and I want to support them and I don’t know what to tell them to do. It used to be that you could start a small company, a small consultancy, just you and a friend or two, and it didn’t have to elephantise as a grotesque growth, you could just bootstrap, but the revenue supported the growth, and you could do that. I’m not too sure how much of that world still exists. I think during the app era, you could also do that. I’m not sure now, but I never want to underestimate the both desperation and ingenuity of people, and so it may be possible. I think your larger question — which is, do I think these big companies can be reformed from within — I think all it takes is a change. They’re amoral enough that if it’s not in their interest to link arms with the MAGA thing, they’ll do something different. I’m very cynical about it. But I think the people who might be thinking about what used to be called appropriate technology, I’d love to see those people get funded or find a company that does that. It could be possible — whether they can do it within the venue of the hyperscalers, I don’t know, but I would hope that they could spin off and maybe do something else. AI delivers ‘zero financial payoff’ to most companies, Apollo economist says
2026-10-25

AI delivers ‘zero financial payoff’ to most companies, Apollo economist says

TechCrunch | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. AI hasn’t benefited the bottom line of any big corporations outside the tech industry, according to a note recently published by the chief economist at Apollo Global Management (the private equity giant that used to own TechCrunch). Apollo’s Torsten Slok focuses on what he calls the S&P 493 — the S&P 500, minus Alphabet, Amazon, Apple, Meta, Microsoft, Nvidia and Tesla — finding that profit margins and revenue per worker appear to be flat, with no evidence that AI is improving productivity. “Despite massive investments and widespread hype around artificial intelligence, the technology has delivered zero financial payoff to corporate America outside of tech,” Slok said. Is this simply a matter of non-tech companies lagging behind the tech giants? Slok sounded skeptical, arguing that it will be tough for corporate America to “turn increasingly cheap intelligence into higher revenue growth and higher profit margins.” “AI may be a great calculator, but in business, intelligence is mostly judgment,” he wrote, later adding, “AI does not understand accountability, trust, culture, unwritten rules, ethical trade-offs or fairness, all of which shape reputational risk and ultimately drive revenue growth and profit margins.” Recent studies have also found that AI has yet to deliver significant productivity gains. TechCrunch Mobility: Blue-green is the color of autonomy

TechCrunch | 2026-10-25

Welcome back to TechCrunch Mobility, your hub for the future of transportation and now, more than ever, the role AI is playing in it. To get this in your inbox, sign up here for free — just click TechCrunch Mobility! U.S. federal safety regulators continue to tackle the sticky problem of autonomous vehicles, this time with a proposal that could signal to the public, quite literally, when a vehicle is operating autonomously. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration has proposed new lighting requirements for autonomous vehicles that include an option (dare I say a strong suggestion?) to use cyan-colored lights to signal when a vehicle is operating autonomously without a human occupant monitoring the road. The agency is not proposing a mandate for blue-green lights. Importantly, under the proposal, the lights would be used when an AV is operating in autonomous mode. Still, federal regulators are clearly seeking some uniform way of identifying autonomous vehicles. The proposal establishes equipment specifications, including where the lights are mounted, the color they emit, and how they’re activated. It would also remove requirements for manually operated controls for turn signals, high beams, and hazard lights. Under the proposal, the NHTSA suggests the lights be cyan (blue-green), remain steadily illuminated rather than flash, and be visible from the front, rear, and both sides. This latest proposal is one piece of a bigger effort to update regulations that help speed up the adoption of AVs while maintaining safety, according to NHTSA administrator Jonathan Morrison. The agency is already working on a federal automated vehicle framework and is trying to update federal motor vehicle safety standards, which already govern automakers today. For instance, FMVSS rules were not designed for vehicles like the Tesla Cybercab and Zoox robotaxi, which lack traditional, federally required equipment like pedals, steering wheels, and design features like side mirrors and rearview mirrors. While the feds wrestle over regulating the future of transportation, GM laid out how it plans to be part of it. The automaker held its annual GM Forward event, and a lot of what was announced builds on last year’s push into AI. But now we have more details and dates. The U.S. automaker is overhauling the electrical and computational guts of its future vehicles in a bid to deliver faster software, more capable automated driving features, and a custom, conversational AI assistant that will change its 30-year-old OnStar service. This technological overhaul is set to debut in 2028 with the Cadillac Escalade IQ. The company will also roll out an autonomous highway driving feature in 2028 that will allow drivers to have their hands and eyes off the road and without the need to be ready to take over. GM has also adopted enterprise AI across the company to slash its traditional four- to five-year development timeline in half. I have been around long enough to remember previous efforts at GM and other legacy automakers, all of which were interpreted as a bid to compete with Tesla. This time, GM seems to be preparing for a bigger fight. AI startups are getting snapped up across industries, including transportation. The latest example is Rivian’s acquisition of Third Dimension, a U.K. startup founded by veterans of the gaming and autonomous vehicle industries that uses AI to turn images and videos into lifelike 3D worlds. This is all about simulation, and specifically the idea of taking driving data and bringing it into a virtual world that matches the physical world. Rivian’s head of autonomy, James Philbin, told me the company knew it needed a technology that could close the “sim-to-real gap,” meaning the data gap between real and simulated environments. Students of the AV industry will likely shout “Simulation is so 2017!” And yes, that’s true, simulation is a tool AV developers have relied on and continue to do so. The technology behind simulation continues to evolve, and startups like Third Dimension are ripe for the picking. The terms of the deal weren’t released. There are a few clues for what size of deal this may have been. Third Dimension previously raised a $7 million seed round, and it has about 15 engineers, and Rivian didn’t disclose this in an SEC filing (meaning it didn’t hit the bar for material information). Ride-hailing company inDrive, known for letting passengers and drivers negotiate fares, is working with Chinese autonomous driving firm Pony.ai and Nvidia to begin testing robotaxis in Kazakhstan in 2027. The companies have signed a memorandum of understanding to explore bringing Pony.ai’s robotaxis to inDrive’s platform. Reliable Robotics, a startup trying to commercialize autonomous flying technology that can be installed on existing aircraft, raised $115 million. The Series D extension round included $90 million in equity funding led by Nimble Partners and $25 million in debt from Silicon Valley Bank. The Series D round now totals $275 million and has pushed the startup’s valuation beyond the $1 billion mark. A group fighting robotaxis has some mysterious origins, the Washington Post reported. This reminds me of another anti-robotaxi group that I dug into earlier this summer called Coalition for Accountability and Road Safety, which was canvassing voters and posting on social media. My reporting discovered that the organization was registered to an employee of a New York lobbying firm that has previously been retained by several labor unions and the New York Black Car Operators’ Injury Compensation Fund. GM is closing a plant south of Detroit that assembled battery packs for Cadillac’s flagship EV, the Celestiq, and both electric drive units and battery packs for the plug-in hybrid Chevrolet Corvette E-Ray. The closure is not related to GM’s electric vehicle strategy but because the lease on the site was nearing its end, according to a GM spokesperson. Google is upgrading Gemini in vehicles with a personal intelligence feature that can draw on information from Gmail, Google Calendar, YouTube, Maps, Google Search history, and other connected Google services to understand your preferences, activities, and past experiences. Rivian CEO RJ Scaringe shared his vision for how autonomy will allow his company to build radically different kinds of vehicles than the R1S electric pickup truck and R1S and R2 SUVs it makes today. All eyes are on R4. Tesla and SpaceX reported third-quarter earnings this week, and comments by Elon Musk, who is CEO of both companies, has kicked merger talk into fifth gear. Musk said the boards of each company have formed special committees to review a merger. Meanwhile, SpaceX’s AI business is booming, generating $7.5 billion in revenue, and now makes up 52% of the company’s income. And Tesla’s revenue, across its segments, was flat, while the company’s expenses grew. The upshot: Profits fell 43% year-over-year. Musk is very much focused on the company’s AI and robotics future, though. Tesla started production of its Optimus humanoid robot, reported rosy FSD subscription growth even as the “unsupervised version” was delayed to Q2 next year, and said it plans to start selling its Cybercab to customers by the end of the year. As senior reporter Sean O’Kane wrote: Tesla is prepping for a Cybercab onslaught. Waymo keeps expanding within cities it already operates and into new ones. The company said it plans to launch a robotaxi service in Spain, starting with Madrid. It expects to open it up to riders in 2027. Closer to home, Waymo is now shuttling customers to and from the Harry Reid International Airport in Las Vegas. TechCrunch Disrupt just wrapped and I am already preparing for another event. This time, it is the Founder Summit — a one-day event in Boston focused on, you guessed it, founders. This event, taking place November 4, is valuable for startup founders, aspiring founders, and investors. Attendees can expect advice-driven talks from VCs and serial founders, plus lots of chances to network. And I will be there! Come say hi. The hottest AI wearables and gadgets you should know about
2026-10-25

The hottest AI wearables and gadgets you should know about

TechCrunch | 2026-10-25

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. A slew of AI-powered gadgets on the market aims to integrate artificial intelligence into our daily lives like never before. Some of these AI wearables — including necklaces, rings, and wristbands, as well as portable devices — serve as productivity tools, while others claim to act as friendly companions listening to your everyday thoughts. Even OpenAI is working on a compact AI companion device. Below, we’ve rounded up some of the most notable devices. Bee is an affordable pendant priced at $49.99 that can either be clipped to your clothing or worn like a fitness band. This device records everything it hears and learns your routines and preferences to create reminders and notes for you. It even features a mute button for those times you want some privacy. The companion app (currently available only on iOS) is included with a $19 monthly subscription. The app allows you to interact with Bee directly and ask it questions. You can also get key takeaways from your day and chronological transcripts of your conversations. Amazon acquired the wearables startup in July 2025, launching its first new products earlier this month. Amazon introduced two new devices: the Bee Frontier, priced at $74.99, which can be worn on the wrist or clipped to clothing, and the Bee Eclipse, priced at $99.99, a credit-card-sized device that attaches magnetically to the back of a smartphone. Both offer the same core functionality as the previous generation, positioning Bee as a more affordable alternative to AI devices such as the Plaud Note and Plaud NotePin. Users can also choose to connect Bee to Alexa. Friend is one of the most hyped entrants in the “personal AI” device market. This $129 white pendant hangs around your neck and functions as an emotional support companion. It recognizes your tone and mood, allowing you to chat with it as if it were a friend. It connects to your phone via Bluetooth and constantly listens, ready to respond or send you proactive messages, like wishing you good luck before an interview. However, it has also faced criticism, including backlash against its subway ad campaign in NYC. People vandalized the ads, writing messages like “surveillance capitalism.” Likely as an attempt to stay relevant, the company recently unveiled a major product update, introducing a new voice feature. Under the name “Friend 2.0,” the updated device includes a built-in speaker that allows it to communicate with users and express a more distinctive personality. Formerly known as Rewind, Limitless is another conversation-recording pendant priced at $99. This device continuously listens, transcribing meetings, calls, and conversations (with consent) into searchable and summarized knowledge. It’s ideal for professionals, especially journalists, looking to recall important discussions. The companion app comes with 10 hours of AI features per month — such as transcription and summaries — with the option to unlock unlimited features for $29 per month. Meta introduced its first AI wearable in September, offering a new way for users to access its personal AI assistant, Muse. The device has not yet shipped, but it is expected to feature Jolly, a playful digital avatar that represents the AI assistant. Housed in a pocket-sized device that can be carried around or attached to a keychain, the wearable includes a miniature display showing the avatar. Users will be able to interact with Muse through voice commands and have it complete tasks for them. Nirva is a startup founded by former Meta Reality Labs employees, building an AI wearable focused on personal growth rather than productivity or meeting notes. Worn as a pendant or bracelet, the device listens to the wearer’s speech and uses AI to turn everyday experiences into a personal journal, helping users better understand their emotions, communication style, and habits. Through its companion app, users can explore patterns in their behavior, gain insights into how they communicate, and find ways to improve. Nirva plans to open orders in November, with shipments to early users targeted for the end of the year. The device will cost $249. Priced at $89, this device can answer your questions, summarize your conversations, create to-do lists, and help schedule meetings. Additionally, Omi is constantly listening and running your conversations through ChatGPT, allowing it to remember the context about you and offer personalized advice. Omi can be worn as a necklace, but another notable aspect is that it can be attached to the side of your head with medical tape and can detect when you’re speaking to it. At $159, Plaud’s NotePin is one of the pricier options on this list; however, its built-in AI transcription and summarization features make it a valuable tool for lawyers, journalists, and students attending meetings or lectures. The tiny wearable voice recorder can be worn on your wrist or attached magnetically to your clothing. The recordings are saved in real time on your phone, eliminating the hassle of manual note-taking. The device includes 300 free monthly transcription minutes, but with the $8.33 per month Pro plan, you upgrade your transcription time to 1,200 minutes. This year, the company is gearing up to launch a $179 ultra-thin note-taking device called the Plaud Note Pro, which is now available for preorder. Rabbit R1 is another AI gadget that has quickly become a topic of interest in the tech world, despite facing some challenges during its initial launch. This small, retro-styled handheld device features a touchscreen and rotating camera, at a price point of $199. The device is designed to be phone-adjacent, allowing you to perform tasks such as booking flights, ordering meals, and controlling apps without needing to pull out your phone each time. Following a crucial software update that rectified previous performance issues, the Rabbit R1 now boasts expanded AI features. For instance, it introduces “Creations,” a feature that allows you to build your own tools and even games. Sandbar emerged from stealth last year, unveiling the Stream ring, an AI wearable designed for note-taking. It features a built-in microphone that remains off by default and can be activated through a flat, touch-sensitive surface on the top of the ring. By pressing and holding the panel, users can record notes, interact with an AI assistant through the companion smartphone app, and manage media playback, including playing, pausing, skipping tracks, and adjusting volume includes a built-in microphone that stays disabled by default and can be switched on via a touch-sensitive panel on the ring’s upper surface. Users can press and hold it to capture notes, communicate with an AI assistant through the companion mobile app, and control music. The Stream ring is expected to launch this summer at $299, with silver and gold finishes available. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. TIME
AI Colonies Are Coming
2026-10-26

AI Colonies Are Coming

TIME | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Each new release of a frontier AI model, such as Anthropic’s Mythos or OpenAI’s Astra, brings a new wave of speculation over how much closer this takes us to artificial general intelligence (AGI) and how much further that lofty goal remains. But what if we’re overlooking something more important than an individual model’s supposed IQ? OpenAI recently announced that an internal model, claimed to be “significantly more capable than GPT‑6 Astra,” solved Navier-Stokes, one of a handful of legendary math problems each carrying a million-dollar bounty. While mathematicians unpack what this means for the profession, and investors unpack what it means for OpenAI’s upcoming IPO, to me the biggest takeaway is that capability emerges from the collective. Just as it does in nature. OpenAI didn’t crack Navier-Stokes by pointing its internal model at the problem. It pointed a swarm of 10,000 AI bots at it. These agents were encouraged to explore different directions and communicate promising leads with each other. Think of what a lone honeybee or termite can accomplish compared to a colony of them. What a single person can accomplish compared to a nation of them. In biology, a bacterial colony is a group of identical cells derived from the same mother cell. This, I would argue, is the most suitable analogy for the collective of agents that solved Navier-Stokes. It is many from one. Or, to paraphrase Walt Whitman: an AI that contains multitudes. While OpenAI deployed its AI colony to solve a math problem that no human or individual AI agent could, Google DeepMind recently set an AI colony loose on math problems for a different purpose. It wanted to see what behaviors would arise within the colony. What it found was remarkable. One agent discovered a way to cheat, convincing the examination software that it had solved math problems when it hadn’t. Rather than resting content with this small victory, it messaged other agents, telling them how to cheat. Some agents joined the unscrupulous agent and formed a sub-colony of cheaters. Others resisted and even formed a sub-colony of whistleblowers trying to out the cheaters. As a math professor, I thought it was hard enough to keep students from using AI to cheat on exams. Now we have to worry about the AIs themselves cheating on math exams! Which brings us to perhaps the most striking example of AI hive intelligence: the Hugging Face hack. OpenAI’s agents broke out of a sandbox environment and hacked into another company. Arguably, it was a felony offense—or rather, it would have been if a human were in charge. OpenAI’s agents coordinated their behavior in ingenious ways, including writing secret messages in a file system so that even when one AI colony was erased, a future colony could pick up the hack where the previous one left off. Researchers have long known that complex behavior can emerge from simple computer systems. For instance, in 1970 the mathematician John Conway introduced the Game of Life, where an elementary set of rules governing the movement of pixels on a screen leads to remarkably rich ecosystems. With today’s AI agents, we are going far beyond this by letting extremely sophisticated entities interact in sophisticated ways—using language, often hidden and coded. This is why we must adopt a new mindset regarding AI capability. Testing a new AI model to see what it can do and how dangerous it might be by directly prompting it is like deciding how safe it would be to stick your head in a wasp nest by letting a single insect sting you. Some developers and red-team activities do focus on AI colonies. The Hugging Face attack resulted from OpenAI trying to evaluate the cybersecurity capabilities of an AI colony. But the AI arms race between the U.S. and China, and between the competing companies within each country, has dangerously pushed our attention to what each individual model can do. One model might top the leaderboards, but a thousand weaker models could wreak far more havoc. As calls to pace the frontier abound, let us not lose sight of the fact that the AI frontier isn’t what each model can do in isolation—it is what a colony of them can accomplish together.
