In Iowa, the Future of American Politics Is Happening Right Now
2026-10-26

In Iowa, the Future of American Politics Is Happening Right Now

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. There will be a time soon when Donald Trump is a historic figure and the 20th century is an increasingly hazy memory. If you want to know what that next era of politics will look like, you don’t have to wait. You can go to Iowa, where the gubernatorial election is a window into the future of politics for decades to come. The millennial-on-millennial contest between Democrat Rob Sand and Republican Zach Lahn is one of the country’s most competitive, despite taking place in what was once a prototypical swing state but has shifted hard toward the GOP in the past decade. Public polls have consistently shown Sand leading—even Lahn acknowledged to reporters that “I think we’re probably down because of the midterm headwinds”—but no one in Iowa politics from either party expects either candidate to win handily. After all, there’s concern about the accuracy of polling in the state where Ann Selzer, its top pollster, showed Kamala Harris winning on the eve of the 2024 election. Trump won by 13 percent, and Selzer, who previously had a stellar track record in polling, promptly retired. But regardless of who wins, this race is a preview of what the 2030s and 2040s will look like. Sand has tapped into the Democratic resentment toward all politics as usual, Lahn is working toward a synthesis of the populist impulses of Trumpism and the conservative ideology that came before it. It’s a sneak peek of a time when millennials will dominate our politics and the political divides that have dominated since the baby boomers came of age will become increasingly obsolete. Politics has long been oriented along the battle lines of a prosperous, optimistic United States that was successfully fighting communism abroad and consistently producing economic prosperity at home. That’s not the world of today. Both Sand and Lahn entered the job market at the time of the Great Recession and are now trying to appeal to an Iowa electorate that has long since been ravaged by deindustrialization—and is currently trying to cope with potential dislocations from A.I. It is a world in which print newspapers are increasingly an anachronism and voters get their information from social media. It is a world that has already seen the rise of unconventional politicians ranging from Donald Trump to Zohran Mamdani and is likely to even further depart from the norms of the late 20th century moving forward. After spending a week traveling across Iowa, talking to voters and following both gubernatorial candidates, I think it’s clear how quickly that era is approaching. Rob Sand was long considered the star of the Iowa Democratic Party’s bench, but by the time this election cycle started, he wasn’t just the star of the bench; he was the only one there. Races in Iowa hadn’t been that close recently. It wasn’t just that Trump won handily in 2024 but that incumbent Gov. Kim Reynolds won by nearly 20 points in 2022 as the last Democrat in Iowa’s congressional delegation lost, along with the Democratic state attorney general and state treasurer, who each had spent 40 years in office. The only statewide Democrat left was Sand, who won reelection as state auditor by just under 3,000 votes, in part because of a strenuous work ethic that included an annual tour of all 99 counties in the Hawkeye State. Sand has long cultivated a moderate image and played up pursuits like his love of hunting and predilection for gas station breakfast pizza. A lanky 44-year-old who painted himself as an earnest advocate for taxpayer efficiency, Sand has tried to conjure up a public perception as half Atticus Finch, half youth pastor. At a visit to a food bank, he earnestly, and without prompting, shares with the volunteers his thoughts on how they can best make peanut butter and jelly sandwiches (peanut butter on both sides of the bread, jelly in between). On the stump, Sand espouses what even he acknowledges is anti-politics, a deep disdain for the two-party system and American political institutions in general. It’s an nonideological message that harks back to Jimmy Carter as much as any modern politician. Just as Carter was a reaction to a country reeling from Watergate, Sand has an approach that appeals not just to Republicans jaded by Trump and a decade of GOP dominance in Iowa but to Democrats scornful of a political establishment that has allowed Trump and a decade of GOP dominance to happen. His events are studded with signs featuring his campaign slogan: Not redder or bluer, but better and truer.” Speaking to a crowd at Grinnell College, the elite liberal arts institution located in a small pocket of white-collar prosperity in central Iowa, Sand gave a withering critique of modern American politics. “Does anybody here think that politics is just peachy these days? No. “Does anybody here think that either the Democratic or the Republican Party, the leadership of either, there are a bunch of heroes that are just doing a bang-up job of solving all of our problems? “The idea itself is kind of laughable, isn’t it?” For Sand, both parties consisted of a failed duopoly. “We have two private clubs who are more interested in beating up on each other than they are in actually solving ordinary people’s problems or making ordinary people’s lives better.” He added, “In fact, a lot of times they are perfectly willing to use ordinary people in order to maintain power by making some group of ordinary people scared of some other, usually smaller group of ordinary people, right? And if they can exploit that fear enough, then they and their friends can pick the pockets of ordinary people while maintaining power. Does anybody think that that’s an unfair description of politics today?” There was no dissent. This resentment of the “political establishment” has increasingly become de rigueur in a Democratic Party where candidates often compete in their disdain for traditional party institutions. Among the party’s most popular figures are Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, who first rose to fame by successfully primarying a member of House Democratic leadership, and Bernie Sanders, who is not even a Democrat. Yet while both Ocasio-Cortez and Sanders are on the left of the party, this disdain is nonideological, as Democrats across the ideological spectrum increasingly disapprove of party leadership. The anti-leadership frustration is perhaps to be expected, given that the status quo has led to two terms of Donald Trump. It’s also resonating on the stump. Sand was standing on a campus that had long been a die-hard stronghold for progressive Democrats. But as he held forth about the failures of both parties, students in the room nodded their heads in unison. Speaking to Slate, Sand insisted he disdained the party system despite the fact that he not only was a two-time statewide elected official as a Democrat but had worked on Democratic campaigns in his youth. “I registered as an independent when I first registered to vote because I don’t really like either political party. That doesn’t mean that you’re not active in it,” he said. “Even though I’ve been a participant in the two-party system, I’m still someone that has always looked at it and said, like, why? Why do we do it this way? Doesn’t make any sense. And so, I just think that people appreciate that. And if it’s the right attitude for the moment, great. But I would be talking about it even if it wasn’t.” Some of Sand’s approach is tailored to his red-state run. Indeed, even as he runs for governor as a Democrat, he’s playing up what he expects would be checks on his own power. He has long buffeted his appeal to independents and Republicans by noting that Iowa’s huge Republican legislative majorities mean it is beyond unlikely that Democrats will gain control of one, let alone both legislative chambers in Des Moines. He didn’t even seem to want a scenario in which Democrats would win a majority when asked by Slate. “I genuinely believe that most of the good stuff is in divided control,” he said. “What an ideal would be is if you had both chambers with razor-thin majorities, where you actually could have people go one way or the other.” Sand may go further than most Democrats in his seeming indifference as to whether his own party wins legislative majorities in the Hawkeye State, but this is an indication of an aversion to traditional politics that is becoming stronger within the Democratic Party. It’s not just that the party is perceived to be a failure by many of its voters; it’s that it is actively loathed by a good portion of its membership. The belief that the party has become an empty vessel is one of the rare articles of faith shared across ideological factions. The institutions that were the base of the Democratic Party, like trade unions and Black churches, are shrinking and leaving nothing to replace them except the corrosive nature of social media. Instead, Sand offers his own simple solutions, a world where we put away our phones—he describes social media as “the scourge of our times: It’s shortening our attention spans, it’s filling our heads with lies, and it’s making us hate people we love”—and just sit down, have coffee with each other, and find common-sense answers to our problems. On policy, Sand sticks carefully to the center. He opposes transgender athletes competing in women’s sports and focuses his opposition on a controversial school voucher program recently introduced in the state, noting the wasteful spending it entails, as well as what he considers the unneeded subsidies it provides for well-off Iowans. He even dodges on whether he backs a constitutional amendment on the ballot, backed by Republicans, that would require legislative supermajorities to ever raise personal or corporate income taxes in the future. “Now, making it harder to raise taxes on ordinary people, to me, sounds like a good idea, but I don’t know why they’re including making it harder to raise taxes on corporations at the same time,” he told a crowd in a coffee shop in Davenport, Iowa. Instead, he focuses on issues like water quality, a topic that has become central in a state where the cancer rate is skyrocketing and many people have become reluctant to drink the tap water, and pushes for systematic reforms to electoral politics, like nonpartisan primaries and term and age limits for elected officials. This attitude even appealed to attendees at a debate watch party that the Sand campaign held in Davenport for the first gubernatorial debate in early October. It was a crowd of Democratic faithful where local candidates pressed the flesh and attendees sitting down next to each other compared notes on what they did at the John Deere factory in the blue-collar river town. Alan Lavine, of Bettendorf, told Slate that he liked Sand’s “distrust of the two-party system. I like that he talks about that. It’s one of the main things that I like.” Lavine was a partisan Democrat but was hesitant to say whether he’d show up for a debate party for Josh Turek, the party’s Senate nominee, who was running a more traditional campaign in a toss-up race. Republicans disdain Sand as a pious hypocrite who is simply the creation of an overly friendly media and his wife’s family wealth. After all, his wife comes from one of the wealthiest families in the state—it has spent eight figures funding his campaign this year—and Sand has long made himself accessible to national reporters. It’s hard to think of a major outlet where the Iowa auditor has not gone on a hunting trip and scarfed down breakfast pizza with a reporter. If Sand falls short of the governor’s mansion, it’ll be in part because Zach Lahn punctures that aura. But that’s not all the Republican is running on. Iowa Republican politics had long been defined by a conflict between a powerful establishment and a social conservative opposition that predated Trump. The state’s battle lines don’t cleanly fall along the MAGA–vs.–non-MAGA feuds of other states. Lahn represents neither faction and ran in the primary as a comparative outsider (although he eventually became the anti-establishment favorite as he bested the Trump-endorsed candidate, Congressman Randy Feenstra). This was helped by the fact that although he is an Iowa native, he had spent much of his adult life living outside the state before returning in 2023 (a fact that Sand has made a key issue of in his campaign, spending millions of dollars to attack his Republican opponent as a carpetbagger from Kansas). Instead, he represents a deeply Trump-influenced alternative. His politics can be best described as common-good conservatism, a philosophy that represents the explicit use of state power for right-wing ends. The Make America Healthy Again–tinged Lahn sees a real role for the state to achieve conservative goals rather than deferring to the free market. It is the fusionist politics advocated by J.D. Vance when he was in the Senate and still by Sen. Josh Hawley, a Missouri Republican. It’s a long, long way from the Reagan-Bush era GOP’s view of economic policy—and represents an evolution even from the second Trump administration. On the stump, Lahn says the key issue of his campaign is to “protect and preserve the culture of Iowa.” The Republican candidate noted that places like Illinois and Minnesota, particularly Minneapolis, lost this battle to “defend their culture.” There’s no dog whistling here; Lahn has repeatedly been explicit that when he talks about culture, he means one grounded firmly in Christianity. (Unlike many other Republicans fighting the culture war, Lahn doesn’t even bother with the Judeo- prefix when discussing these religious roots of American culture.) For him, this was under threat by the specter of the left trying to undermine this in the name of “collectivism.” He pointed to the rise of Mamdani in New York and Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan, who he said was “connected directly with the Muslim Brotherhood,” adding, “This is what it looks like when you let down your guard of defending your culture.” Lahn caveated: “We are a welcoming people. We always have been. Iowa has been a welcoming place, but if you don’t understand what built this country, the ideals that built this country, there’s no way you can defend this country.” But he also tied culture to fighting powerful private interests as well. Speaking in rural Iowa, he described fighting eminent domain as a cultural issue. “So many of your families in this room came to Iowa probably in 1850, 1860, 1870, 1880. Why did they come here? They didn’t come here for religious freedom, because we already had it in Europe. The Reformation had happened. … What did they not have? They did not have their land. They couldn’t pass something down to their kids. A very small group of wealthy, land-owning, and elite controlled everything.” He added: “By the way, does that sound familiar to how things are going across our country right now in agriculture?” This leads to Lahn going places that are explicitly against the traditional free-market-based beliefs of the Republican Party of the late 20th century. He scorns what he describes as “market fundamentalism” and told reporters: “We don’t worship the market. Our faith is the foundation of our state.” In particular, one of the targets of his ire has been “unchecked monopolies in the agricultural industry”; he added that, in his view, “monopoly is a failure in the free market.” Lahn rails against industry consolidation and argues that this was in part what inspired the American Revolution. “The East India Company basically ran England, and [the Founding Fathers] knew what it was like to have an out-of-control monopoly.” Speaking to Slate, Lahn denounced big business and consolidated power. “Big business buys off both sides. You look at some of the biggest companies; they donate to both sides, and what do they do that for? So we will turn a blind eye. Well, they’re not donating to me,” he insisted. One example he pointed to was lab-grown meat. “Why is it legal in Iowa? Many states have banned it, but it’s an assault on the cattle industry. “Who invested $100 million into lab-grown meat? JBS,” Lahn said, taking aim at the Brazilian-based conglomerate that is the largest meatpacking company in the United States. “The people that are processing meat in one of the big three are also in the market of finding other solutions and not working with farmers to undermine farmers.” He added: “And it’s not just them. There’s many of these groups doing it.” At times Lahn almost sounded like Sand, including when he was asked about the tension of running as an insurgent from the incumbent party. “It’s just that, you know, this has been the most frustrating thing for me running for office, is just that everything is boiled down to this lowest common denominator of: What letter do you have by your name?” However, he quickly disagreed that there was any commonality. “[Sand] is doing that because he’s just trying to message independents, but he’s not actually trying to solve the problem of politicians being bought off.” Lahn’s message draws a range of attendees to his events. In Jefferson, Iowa, Tim Heisterkamp was a loyal Republican who voted for Lahn in the primary but was particularly concerned about keeping the state red. “I just don’t think going backwards with the progress that we’ve made in certain areas is worth it. Fiscally, we are as sound as we’ve ever been, and I think we need to keep it that way.” Heisterkamp was also concerned that Sand would roll back the state’s new school voucher program. In Indianola, Bob Cooper, a bald and bearded attendee, was simply worried about the state of the country. “Muslims are taking over,” he said. “It’s already slipping in now. Like I tell everybody all the time, how do you eat an elephant? One bite at a time, you know. So that’s what’s happened. We’ve accepted this.” Cooper had not always been a Republican. He grew up in a Democratic family and spent the ’70s as a “hippie” and “not doing much voting.” However, he had come to appreciate that “a lot of good people died so that we can have the freedom that we have. And every day I thank God that I was born in a country where we have freedom. Look at France right now, for instance. The only difference between us and them, we have guns. They don’t have guns.” Iowa is not likely to be mistaken for France anytime soon, but Lahn’s version of conservatism is still very European. It represents the next step in the transition on the American right in the Trump era to a more populist and more statist worldview. After all, all of Trump’s many heresies from traditional American conservative thought, ranging from his 2016 opposition to entitlement reform to the recent push for the federal government to take ownership stakes in a number of privately owned companies, are far more in tune with the politics of Marine Le Pen than those of Mitt Romney. There’s no indication that there will be any reversion to the GOP of the past. Instead, in the post-Trump era, Republicans will have to seek to emulate what aspects of Trumpism can actually be copied, as opposed to what are unique to a man who was a celebrity for generations before he was a politician. Lahn’s effort, which encompasses both a strain of nativism and real skepticism of corporate power along with many traditional shibboleths of the American right, feels the logical next step in this, an attempt to redefine the GOP around the political coalition that reelected Trump in 2024 and move beyond the political landscape of the past. Of course, the stakes in the race include not just the governor’s mansion and which millennial will be an ideological flag bearer for their party moving forward but potentially a Senate seat. Iowa’s Republican incumbent Chuck Grassley is 93 years old. Although Grassley is in good health and has even filed to run for reelection in 2028, he has noted the risk of being replaced by a Democratic governor if he dies in office. When asked how they would deal with a Grassley replacement, both Sand and Lahn agreed that they would consider voting for a Republican. Sand said he would look to “trying to pick someone that’s actually going to represent Iowans, not a party. That might be picking someone who’s independent.” The Democratic candidate even noted that he could think of one Republican he would appoint. “I wouldn’t name them because I don’t want them to get kicked out of the party. I could think of at least one that I think would do a good job.” Lahn pointed to someone who understands “the grassroots.” He added: “You’re going out to Washington, D.C., to fight a machine … the Senate is one place you can actually have a real influence. So I would want somebody that would be willing to take these more populist ideas—economic and health—and carry that on outside of Iowa.” The governor of Iowa always takes on an outsize role in American politics due to the state’s first-in-the-country caucuses. If Lahn wins, he will be there as a kingmaker in 2028 when Republicans go first. And Sand has already pledged that if he wins, he will ensure that the Iowa law that mandates that the Hawkeye State go first is followed even though the Democratic National Committee has attempted to end Iowa’s status as an early state. And of course that will just be amplified because of their relative youth and because a win from either would justly be considered impressive. Sand would be the first Democrat elected governor in 20 years in a state that Trump won three times. If Lahn wins, it would be despite being consistently down in polls and outspent. But what would stand out is how much they would represent the future of their parties. Lahn’s views represent the fusion of Trumpism, inchoate as it may be as an ideology, with the Republican Party of the past. And Sand represents the wholesale rejection among Democrats of party institutions of the past. Although he shares little politically with figures like Zohran Mamdani, there is a commonality in their rejection of their party and efforts to build a politics that rejects the institutions of the past. And regardless of who wins this matchup this year, it is likely to be an indication of the battle lines drawn in political races for decades to come. What if Vivek Ramaswamy Just Ruined Everything for the GOP?
2026-10-24

What if Vivek Ramaswamy Just Ruined Everything for the GOP?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-24

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, a politics newsletter steeling itself for the sheer number of D.C. adults planning to dress as “Jon Husted’s ass” for Halloween. We’re almost there, reader. There are just 10 days until the midterms. After that, and after several years of litigation in Alaska, we may know who controls the Senate. This week, we look at Democrats’ still-awful favorability numbers and ask how much it matters amidst all this. Regrettably, Wedgewood polling is no longer able to survey this for us. Plus: Mississippi?? Let’s begin with this week’s signature unpleasantness. This keeps sounding like hyperbole in our head, but we can’t think of anything that tops it: Ohio GOP gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy’s ad this week is the most odious advertisement we’ve ever seen. It doesn’t hit hard at some element of Democrat Amy Acton’s record. It doesn’t hit hard at some personal shortcoming. It’s not demagoguery stoking some broader prejudice among the electorate. It’s just inexplicable cruelty. The ad features Acton’s birth mother, with whom Acton has been estranged since she was 12, calling her daughter a liar. The backstory, which you’re probably familiar with by now, is that Acton had accused her stepfather of molesting her between the ages of 9 and 12. Her stepfather would ultimately be indicted for rape, but the stepfather jumped bond and fled the state with Acton’s birth mother. This is as repulsive as people get, and Ramaswamy chose to feature this sinister person in a campaign ad. That, in turn, is as repulsive as campaigns get. We are almost certain—not quite 100 percent, regrettably—that this will backfire on Ramaswamy, who apparently wasn’t doing all that great in his race if he chose this path. There’s an element of edgelord-iness among Republican staff and consultants who’ve come up in the Trump era, who’ve seen Trump’s ability to get away with acts of cruelty and believe the power of the dark arts is boundless. But not every candidate is Donald Trump—Ramaswamy, certainly, lacks any trace of charisma—and voters of all stripes generally don’t like ads in which someone is attacked over her history of being sexually abused as a child. Should this backfire meaningfully, we’re interested in the fallout beyond just Ramaswamy’s own race. This is, after all, the biggest political news story in the country less than weeks before an election. And if the ad moves numbers against Ramaswamy, how much of that spills over to the state’s all-important Senate race as well? While the administration—both Donald Trump and J.D. Vance—are standing by Ramaswamy’s airing of the ad, most prominent Ohio Republicans are keeping their distance. Grandfatherly Gov. Mike DeWine said it was “wrong to run this ad” and urged Ramaswamy to take it down. Secretary of State Frank LaRose said he wouldn’t “run an ad like that.” Retired Ohio Republicans, like former Sen. Rob Portman and ex-Rep. Jim Renacci, trashed it. The latter tweeted that “the floor may have just fallen out from under the Republican campaign for Ohio Governor.” And then there’s Sen. Jon Husted, utilizing all the glute strength he can muster to fend off a challenge from Democratic ex-Sen. Sherrod Brown. While Husted is well cocooned by his handlers at this point, his campaign issued a statement in which Husted said he “would not have run that ad.” It’s about the only ad that Husted won’t run. While it’s not the most-discussed Senate contest in national media, the Ohio Senate race is right up there with Texas’ as the most expensive in the country, rapidly closing in on half-a-billion dollars spent. It’s pivotal, as most if not all paths to a Democratic Senate majority run through Ohio. So if Ramaswamy’s ad, aside from nuking his own campaign, were to bring Husted down another point or two? That could cost Husted the race, and Senate Republicans their majority. How karmically just it would be for so much of the blame to fall on Vivek Ramaswamy. What’s going on in the Alaska Senate race? Beats us. Sure, there are polls showing Democrat Mary Peltola with an edge over GOP Sen. Dan Sullivan. But who knows if those are right? Alaska is a state of 27 people who all spend their day in the woods fighting with bears. No “big-city pollster” knows what these people are thinking. But let’s presume the race is as close as all indications beyond polling—ad spending, Trump and Vance visits, etc.—suggest. And let’s say that Senate control hinges on Alaska. We feel the need, then, to adequately prepare you for the amount of focus that could be trained on Alaska after Election Day. First of all, Alaska is a full 900 time zones behind the continental United States, so its counting won’t be done until late. It also has a ranked-choice voting system, meaning that if none of the four candidates on the ballot achieves a majority—likely—the state won’t tabulate final results until Nov. 18. One of the four candidates on the ballot is Dan J. Sullivan, not to be confused with Sen. Dan S. Sullivan. The senator sued to get J. Sullivan off the ballot, calling him a frivolous spoiler intended to sow voter confusion, but the Alaska Supreme Court allowed both Dans to stay on. It’s worth noting, too, that Sen. Sullivan is clearly marked as the incumbent on the ballot. Now the Trump Justice Department is stepping in. It filed a lawsuit this week in Alaska federal court seeking to block the state from certifying “any result that may be impacted by voter confusion” between the Dans “pending an investigation to determine whether votes cast for Daniel J. Sullivan were meant to be cast for Senator Sullivan.” In other words, the DOJ wants to block certification of the state’s Senate race if Sen. Sullivan … loses. It seems like a stretch to us. It smells like one of those things, though, that could hinge on whether it seems like a stretch to Brett Kavanaugh. The New York Times released a smashing poll for Democrats this week that, if correct, would deliver them a wave similar to the 2008 election, when Democrats won the House popular vote by 10.6 points. In a subsequent analysis, though, the Times notes one warning sign within the data: “Today’s poll found that just 28 percent of voters said they were satisfied with the Democratic Party, compared with 36 percent who said the same of the Republican Party.” It’s true that no one likes the dumb Democrats. Especially when surveyed about it: What are you supposed to say when asked by a pollster, “Are you satisfied with the Democratic Party?” Who responds “yes” to such a question? We just don’t think this is that important in 2026. One big reason is that much of the collapse in the Democratic Party’s image post-2024 has come from Democratic voters, who are disappointed with the party for blowing the 2024 election and for not doing enough to combat Trump. As the Times notes, “Many of those dissatisfied with the party are, in fact, Democrats themselves—43 percent of Democrats said they were dissatisfied with their own party.” But guess what the vast majority of those dissatisfied Democrats are going to do in November? Rush to the polls to vote for Democrats, to protest a miserably unpopular Republican president. For an analogous situation: After that 2008 Democratic wave, no one liked the Republican Party. They had 41 Senate seats and 178 House seats and were generally understood to be bums. They got angry and weird and organized the Tea Party and formed a cult around Fox News television host Glenn Beck. And then, in 2010, they won 63 House seats and six Senate seats—not because voters suddenly liked Republicans, but in protest of Democrats’ governance. Republicans, of course, went on to lose the 2012 presidential election, and Democrats will have to find a way to improve their image in the next two years to avoid a similar fate. All of the criticisms they’re making of Trump—whether on the cost of living or immigration enforcement—are things they’ll need to develop coherent, winning policies around heading into 2028 amid vicious factional disputes. Not to mention, they’ll need to find the proper messenger for those policies. Anyhow, that’s a mess for later. For now, Democrats don’t need to be liked to win. • [Googles “Jim Newell” “Surge” “Wedgewood”… phew …] The Surge never did and never would cite Wedgewood Polls, a fake polling outfit that was busted red-handed this week for making up numbers that may have skewed races, funding decisions, or betting markets. When caught, the outfit then tried to cover it up by deeming itself a “social experiment examining how purported polling information could enter and spread through the political information ecosystem.” Oh, it was just a cheeky term paper, was it? We’ll believe it when a fraud investigation is completed. • Those who had the misfortune of following the 2014 midterms will remember when Republican candidates lumped a bunch of things in the news together en route to a blowout. It was the “ISIS terrorists are bringing Ebola across the border in caravans” election. Anyway, we thought of that this week while watching this ad from South Carolina Sen. Tim Scott, in support of Sen. Darline Graham. In the ad, Scott warns that Graham’s Democratic opponent, Annie Andrews, will “hike taxes to bankroll her transgender agenda.” It’s just silly: The transgender agenda is cheap. It’s the mandatory abortions that will require tax hikes. • An interesting one to follow in Maryland (go crabs!): A ballot question this November will loosen the state’s constitutional requirements for drawing congressional districts. In other words, it will make it easier for the state legislature to gerrymander away its remaining Republican representative, Rep. Andy Harris, in the next round of redistricting wars. But even in one of the bluest states in the country, in one of the bluest environments this century, it’s looking like an uphill climb: In a recent (non-Wedgewood) poll, the plan only had 25 percent support, with 53 percent opposing it. Then again, maybe Harris, who is unpleasant, will just lose on his own terms. • All right, fine: Is the Mississippi Senate race a thing? This week, Senate Democrats’ super PAC announced an investment there to boost Democrat Scott Colom against GOP Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith, while former Vice President Kamala Harris campaigned with Colom. Trump, meanwhile, will hold a rally for Hyde-Smith in Mississippi on Monday. So is it really in play? Oh, absolutely—Hyde-Smith was only up 4 in a recent Wedgewood poll! The French Riots Prove an Old Truism That Americans Must Heed.
2026-10-24

The French Riots Prove an Old Truism That Americans Must Heed.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-24

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Hundreds of thousands of people are protesting in the streets. Rioters are looting stores, burning buildings, and attacking police stations. The authorities have cracked down even as opposition politicians criticize the government for failing to quell the discord. That describes what’s been happening in France for the past month as students, activists, and labor unions protest everything from education funding to inflation. But it also describes what happened in the U.S. in the 1960s, when protests over racism, the Vietnam War, and the assassination of Martin Luther King Jr. sometimes escalated into riots. Civil unrest can be justified and has sometimes prompted progress. But when protests boil over into violence, history shows that right-wing politicians often benefit. In 1968, a broad sense of societal disorder after eight years of Democratic leadership helped Richard Nixon win the White House. Now, the riots in France threaten to help Marine Le Pen, who leads the far-right National Rally party, take over the Élysée Palace in next year’s presidential election. Le Pen, who wants to restrict legal immigration, ban Muslims from wearing hijabs, and distance France from the European Union, has been doing her best to turn the unrest to her advantage. “Our country is caught in a vise, held hostage between violence and powerlessness,” she said this week. “In six months, the French people will be able to choose a different path—one of authority and restored security.” It’s not just France and the U.S. either. In the 1920s, brawls between Italian Blackshirts and workers helped Benito Mussolini position his fascist movement as restorers of order. In 2013, deadly clashes between Hindus and Muslims in the Indian state of Uttar Pradesh helped propel Prime Minister Narendra Modi, a Hindu nationalist, to power. In 2020, China’s government used pro-democracy protests—which escalated to include arson, vandalism, and attacks on police—as a pretext to strip away Hong Kong’s political autonomy. And at the risk of violating Godwin’s law, yes, the Nazis themselves exploited the 1933 torching of the German parliament by a Dutch communist to suspend civil liberties and usher in fascism. Research tends to back up the historical pattern. In a 2020 paper, the UC–Berkeley political scientist Omar Wasow concluded that urban riots following King’s murder shifted white voters toward Nixon by enough to win him the presidency. In general, Wasow told me, public disorder tends to help conservatives because their ideology positions them as custodians of the status quo. With Donald Trump’s approval ratings deep underwater, an ABC News/Washington Post/Ipsos poll last week found that crime was the only issue voters trusted Republicans to handle better than Democrats. “A candidate who presents herself as the one who will restore order after a wave of rioting is following a script with a long record of success,” Wasow said of Le Pen. Ideology isn’t all Le Pen has going for her. She’s a member of the parliamentary opposition, which lets her criticize both the rioters (whom she calls “insurrectionary activists”) and her political opponents’ response (“exhausted, disoriented, and seemingly incapable”) without shouldering much responsibility for fixing either problem. Even so, there’s no guarantee that Le Pen’s bid to ride the riots to victory will work. President Emmanuel Macron and other politicians not of the far right have decried the chaos, and the election is still months away. As Wasow and other researchers have found, a heavy-handed police response—like the kind that happened during the Civil Rights Movement and has already happened in France—can engender public sympathy. Le Pen is also an imperfect messenger. Voters have thrice rejected her presidential aspirations, first in 2012 and again in 2017 and 2022. She was convicted last year of embezzlement but reduced her sentence on appeal by enough to run again. (Talk about déjà vu!) But as Americans have proved, voters are sometimes willing to overlook a candidate’s rap sheet if he or she promises to fix their biggest problems. Le Pen is practiced at trying to turn popular unrest to her advantage. As the journalist Enda O’Doherty relates in a new history of the French far right, Le Pen once counseled members of her own party to publicize a 2007 riot in the town of Saint-Dizier, during which young people clashed with police and set cars ablaze. “Outrage is something that has to be organized,” Le Pen said at the time. She has led polls of the next presidential election for months, and her instinct to frame politics in us-vs.-them terms might well carry the day. “If the choices before the people are presented as order vs. chaos,” O’Doherty told me by email, “I think they will choose order.” The outcome in France will matter on its own terms. But Le Pen’s response to the riots is also a cautionary tale for Americans, who are likely approaching a season of (even deeper) political angst. In the waning days of an ugly midterm season, the Trump administration is already laying the groundwork to contest results it doesn’t like. If Democrats do retake Congress, 2027 will accelerate the political death throes of a president who has toyed with declaring a national emergency and has a history of stoking violence. Many protests, like the “No Kings” rallies that have popped up throughout the president’s second term, are peaceful and patriotic demonstrations of dissent. Perhaps the most immediate lesson of France? If you want to help Trump, throw a rock. Congrats! You’ve made it to the end of another workweek. To honor the fruits of your labor, why not dig into a few tasty stories about the strange and surprising links between (literal) fruit and money? Apples: Apple picking is perhaps the quintessential autumnal activity. There’s something about an apple orchard that’s just suffused with nostalgia, especially if the trees bear heirloom fruits. “The cultural significance of heirlooms—their story, weaved through generations of family grafting and genealogy—is all fine and dandy, but hasn’t science rendered these older apple varieties obsolete?” Danny Palumbo went on a quest to find out. Pears: As the holiday season rolls around, there are always a few people for whom it is a real challenge to buy gifts. Luckily, Slate features editor Shannon Palus knows the perfect treat for that special someone. Grapes: In the mid-1800s a horticulturist in Massachusetts invented the perfect grape, to much fanfare. His invention became an American triumph—but then it fell into obscurity. Hannah Seo investigates why the iconic Concord grape lost its throne. Lemons: Fruit is a symbol of life, fertility, love, and wholesome goodness. But amid the sweetness, there is some seediness too. Christina Cauterucci digs into the intertwined history of organized crime and fruit, including the establishment of the Sicilian Mafia in response to a lemon farming boom. On that criminally delicious note, go forth and enjoy the weekend! We’ll be back in your inbox on Monday. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. iFixit dismantles Apple's Duo: 'You'd better baby that screen'
2026-10-26

iFixit dismantles Apple's Duo: 'You'd better baby that screen'

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Battery replacement gets the thumbs up, but it all hinges on dust. A conventional phone might be better bet for home repairers Apple's iPhone Duo scores higher than its foldable rivals for repairability, but don't let dust get into its delicate workings. iFixit teardown crew put a spudger and screwdriver to Apple's flagship phone and reached a provisional verdict: its repairable, up to a point, and the batteries can be replaced. The inner display and hinge, though, look daunting. The foldable inner screen is the Duo's party trick, yet reports suggest it isn't going smoothly for every owner. Some describe dead-pixel lines around the hinge, and others see horizontal lines on the right-hand side of the display. iFixit's analysis point to the Duo's clever hinge as a possible culprit, rather than a straightforward broken cable. "The damage we're seeing in photos isn't right where the display cable passes through the hinge, but slightly lower, at a point where several edges meet and change how the thin display is supported as it bends," iFixit said. There's a center support bar which, the team speculates, might not be dropping far enough. The result is stress on the screen, near to where it reportedly breaks. As for how that could happen, iFixit dropped the Duo into a bag of glowing powder, and opened and closed it a few times. "The hinge," they said, "started crunching." To be clear, the Duo performed better than rival brands' foldables in terms of dust ingress, however, there was still enough in there to make the hinge crunch. Removing that flexible display to clean it isn't for the faint-hearted. The trim around the screen wasn't much trouble, and the thin film behind the Duo's seemingly creaseless appearance peeled off fairly easily. Removing the inner screen itself, though, took heat and a pick, and was far from easy. The outer screen was much easier to remove, and accessing the batteries (there are two) was relatively straightforward, we're told. The teardown team applied voltage, and the adhesive released the power packs. The cameras, logic boards, and other components were similarly simple to extract, although iFixit notes that five different screwdriver types were needed to finish the teardown. Aside from the inner display and hinge, the Duo is relatively repairable. "Replacing them takes a lot of work," iFixit noted, "and the price could put owners off repairing the phone at all." With the kind of money the Duo demands, AppleCare+ coverage looks like a sensible safeguard, especially given the trickle of screen-failure reports. "For a first attempt, Apple has done well," iFixit observed, having provisionally given the device five out of ten, its highest score yet for a foldable. Conventional phones, such as the latest Fairphone, have, however, scored far higher. "You’d better baby that screen, Duo owners," concluded iFixit, "And maybe quit carrying around that pocket sand." ® Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Aiven hopes to do for LLMs what it does for databases PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good Techies tiptoed around server to keep it working as company sank Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good New embedding, parsing, and reranking models promise smarter agentic RAG workflows Containerized datacenters, androids, and telecoms all feature in Berlin keynote interrupted by environmental protesters GPUzilla boasts modular bit barns could speed up deployments from six months to just two weeks PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Britain bets big on AI to defend against the threats AI is helping create
2026-10-26

Britain bets big on AI to defend against the threats AI is helping create

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree The UK government is throwing £130 million at AI-powered cyber defenses, including plans to deploy autonomous AI agents across critical infrastructure to hunt down security flaws before attackers get there first. The money is part of a wider £600 million-plus AI spending spree announced by the Treasury ahead of this week's Budget, with ministers also promising to tackle biological threats, bankroll British AI firms, and reduce the country's dependence on foreign technology. Of the cyber funding, £60 million will go towards a new National Cyber Shield that uses agentic AI to bolster Britain's defenses. That includes a £25 million challenge fund for UK cybersecurity firms and plans for a regulatory sandbox to test autonomous security tools across critical infrastructure, government and industry. Another £70 million will establish the National Exposure and Real-Time Vulnerability (NERV) Centre, using AI to sniff out vulnerabilities across Britain's infrastructure and wider economy. The announcement comes just days after the PHP2Shell security scare, which saw dozens of government websites taken offline after critical flaws were discovered in the widely used scripting language. Britain's National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) worked with OpenAI to coordinate disclosure of the PHP flaws and give affected vendors time to prepare fixes, after AI-assisted researchers uncovered the vulnerabilities. Whitehall has already been experimenting with AI-powered vulnerability hunting. AI minister Kanishka Narayan said last week that the technology had uncovered 500 previously unknown security flaws across significant parts of the public sector. It remains unclear how much freedom the new AI defenders will have to act without human intervention, or when they'll be deployed. Ciaran Martin, former head of the NCSC, welcomed the investment, pointing to the week's events as evidence that Britain needs to get its cyber defenses in order. "We need to padlock our systems, and harness AI for defense," he said. The Treasury is also handing another £120 million to the AI Security Institute (AISI), which tests advanced AI systems for dangerous capabilities and researches ways to keep them under control. Cybersecurity isn't the only threat keeping ministers busy. Another £165 million will fund a National AI Biosecurity Initiative, including £100 million for what the government claims will be the world's first dedicated AI Biosecurity Lab. The independent, non-profit lab will use frontier AI models to detect biological threats and accelerate the development of vaccines and treatments. OpenAI is contributing £100 million in compute and model credits, while Anthropic is offering up to £100 million in model credits, model access and technical support. Google DeepMind and British biotech firm Basecamp Research are also involved, while the remaining £65 million will go towards biosurveillance and medical countermeasures against dangerous pathogens. There's something for the homegrown AI industry, too. The government is adding £60 million to its Sovereign AI Fund to invest in British companies developing technologies including robotics and autonomous systems, plus another £60 million for AI research and development procurement. A further £18 million will fund an Advanced Chip Design Academy, intended to help Britain develop more of its own semiconductor expertise rather than relying quite so heavily on technology designed overseas. The package also includes £30 million for local AI partnerships, £15 million for an AI challenge prize initially focused on healthcare, and £23 million for research into AI's impact on jobs and the economy. Manchester will get a new NCSC AI Cyber Security Centre of Excellence to train government specialists. Additionally, ministers are setting aside £10 million a year to study how AI will reshape employment and industry, including the jobs it might make redundant. The government clearly has high hopes for AI. Whether the technology can live up to them without creating a fresh set of problems is another matter.® Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Aiven hopes to do for LLMs what it does for databases PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good Techies tiptoed around server to keep it working as company sank Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good New embedding, parsing, and reranking models promise smarter agentic RAG workflows Containerized datacenters, androids, and telecoms all feature in Berlin keynote interrupted by environmental protesters GPUzilla boasts modular bit barns could speed up deployments from six months to just two weeks PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Euro open source specialist proposes LLM gateway to ease token bills
2026-10-26

Euro open source specialist proposes LLM gateway to ease token bills

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Aiven hopes to do for LLMs what it does for databases European open source database service provider Aiven says it has saved 80 percent of its AI token costs internally by moving to open-weight models and is building a gateway to help devs swap models in and out as required. The Finland-based company built its business on hosting and providing open-source databases as a service, and in the last couple of years has moved into machine learning. Speaking to The Register, Ville Lehto, Aiven’s VP of strategy, said that when dealing with open source AI infrastructure, it's not trivial to make something that can run across the different clouds. However, in giving its developers access to LLMs, the company wanted to manage its own token costs and control its "own destiny when it comes to AI usage." After a wave of debate around so-called token-maxxing, developers have generally become more interested in reducing their costs by using a mix of models, including cheaper Chinese alternatives to the Western frontier models, and open-weight models which come at a lower cost. Open-weight models have publicly released learned parameters, principally their weights and biases, and can be run locally or hosted at lower cost than the frontier models from OpenAI and Anthropic. Examples include the Llama Family from Meta, Qwen Family from Alibaba Cloud, China’s DeepSeek, Mistral AI’s Medium 3.5 and Devstral and, Google's Gemma. "When it comes to this token use, as we witnessed early on this year, there are a fraction of users that are absolutely heavy users, and when it comes to their spend, a lot of it could be saved by actually using open-weight models," Lehto said. He said Aiven faced a choice to either have strict token budgets or try to find a more efficient way of using tokens. Opting for the latter, it implemented an internal gateway to help developers work with open-weight models more easily, Lehto said. "We wanted to make it as frictionless as possible. We can offer our employees different open-weight models, be it Western models or the ones from Asia, and take on the frontier models. We've been focusing on the heavy users, and with them we see roughly 80 percent decline in spend." However, since the performance of open-weight and frontier models varies, it was also important to use other metrics, such as the cost per task, he said. Aiven built its gateway around the open source framework called LiteLLM, which it hosted. It added new features such as budgeting, access management, and added other open-source components. It runs the models on rented GPUs and self-hosts the models as well. Lehto said Aiven continued to use frontier models but only to help plan work. "When it comes to actually doing things, we use a lot of open-weight models. The majority of the work is actually done with open-weight, but the most critical bits of the work are planned with frontier models. That's how we see most success at the moment." Aiven plans to offer the gateway as a service to customers to help them manage token costs by making it easier to swap open-weight models in and out of their workflows. "Since our heritage is in open source, it's something that we feel very natural… offering as a service for people in the sense that it also fits the enterprise needs." Aiven's customers include German ecommerce company Conrad, UK payment processing company Dojo, cyber security vendor Sophos, and communications firm Avaya. It is set to be just one of the vendors positioning themselves to try to help organizations reduce the costs when using AI. ® Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Aiven hopes to do for LLMs what it does for databases PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good Techies tiptoed around server to keep it working as company sank Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good New embedding, parsing, and reranking models promise smarter agentic RAG workflows Containerized datacenters, androids, and telecoms all feature in Berlin keynote interrupted by environmental protesters GPUzilla boasts modular bit barns could speed up deployments from six months to just two weeks PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' PHP2Shell is a warning shot for vulnerability disclosure
2026-10-26

PHP2Shell is a warning shot for vulnerability disclosure

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again The technical details of web server stacks are worth learning about. Preferably not from the TV news. Last week, knowing about PHP was a niche professional interest. This week, cab drivers have opinions. PHP is not the story; it's the illustration. There are good reasons that it has this most unfortunate of spotlights. It's very old, and it shows. Growing from roots in the mid-1990s, it has never suffered from the straitjacket of a specification, and while it has seen a lot of refactoring and modernization, it retains design elements from more than 20 years ago. PHP is emphatically not the only project where urgent demands for better performance, reliability, and features consume the resources needed to tidy up ugly internals that appear to work just fine. Until, one day, that technical debt falls due. There will be many more days like this. PHP's significance is that it isn't unique. The details don't matter that much, although this annotated comic book version is both fun and granular if you want to learn about it. Now the dynamics of AI-assisted cybersecurity are becoming clearer, defenders can use the tools to uncover dangerous flaws, but motivated and inventive attackers can use them too. Neither side has a monopoly on resources or ingenuity. The pace and scale of the conflict are growing so quickly that we have to look at how actual global warfare works to reconfigure. The most pertinent aspect of PHP2Shell is the people, not the machines. We know how the LLMs identified the mix of problems and their exploitation over a few days in August, and we know how quickly any public information about such things can be weaponized. It took days, not months, to find, and hours rather than days to exploit. The correct protocol is to disclose patches privately to major, trusted organizations, and then when core services are protected, go public and go hard. But PHP2Shell didn't work out like that, because it was too tasty a treat to keep secret. The first attacks were seen days before the anonymous Hacker News post that announced the presence and severity of the problems. That post had the correct CVE numbers. It came from someone in the loop. The devastating effect of leaked intelligence and the tensions between keeping valuable data secret and being able to use it were abundantly apparent in the Second World War. This led to the system of clearances, classification, and trained liaison officers by which the output of places like Bletchley Park could be filtered, guided, and protected on its way to the people who needed it, and kept away from the eyeballs of those who didn't. It wasn't perfect, and security researchers don't need to work in huts while pretending to be supply clerks, but the lessons learned have proved good to this day. Disclosure needs to be a discipline, not a protocol. It's no good telling an entire IT sec team ahead of time – the absolute minimum number of people should hold the secret for as long as possible. They have to be trained and trusted by the patch developers and their own organizations. If your organization can't field someone with the highest of standards, you'll go down the pecking order of when you're entrusted with the secrets. Patch liaison has to become a hirable position with its place in career structures and qualification requirements. It needs to be part of an industry-wide structure where information can flow both ways.The presence and quality of the person in that role will define where you sit in the classified sequence of deployment. It's a way of managing risk in a very messy, very fast moving, and very consequential part of the IT machine. We need focus, intelligence, and skills just as much as we expect them elsewhere. For all this to work, someone has to manage the whole thing across the industry. Tools and processes need to be developed with the explicit purpose of deploying without disclosure. Hand in hand with that must be a formalized system of identifying, analyzing, and setting in place expeditious factions for the most dangerous infrastructure components, both by deployed base and design nature. There will be a lot of arguments whether PHP can and should be Rusted, for example, but no forum where the debate can be run to a high standard and appropriate resources provided to make the decision happen quickly. Expensive by some standards, staggeringly cheap by others. We've never had to think formally about this as a discipline to be built to professional standards, but we've never had such a combination of complex, powerful attack vectors in a hostile and richly equipped environment. It's serious. It's war. ® Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Aiven hopes to do for LLMs what it does for databases PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good Techies tiptoed around server to keep it working as company sank Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good New embedding, parsing, and reranking models promise smarter agentic RAG workflows Containerized datacenters, androids, and telecoms all feature in Berlin keynote interrupted by environmental protesters GPUzilla boasts modular bit barns could speed up deployments from six months to just two weeks PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' 'Let's just shove a bigger fan in there and see what happens'
2026-10-26

'Let's just shove a bigger fan in there and see what happens'

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Techies tiptoed around server to keep it working as company sank IT people are asked to cram a lot into a working week, which is why The Register tries to ease readers into Monday with a new instalment of “Who, Me?” – our reader-contributed column that shares your stories of office mistakes and escapes. This week, meet a reader we’ll Regomize as "Robert," who told us he worked for an outfit that scours newspapers and websites to find stories mentioning their clients. "We found key words in articles that customers were paying us to monitor for. We would then cut out the articles and send them to the customer," Robert explained. Sadly, business wasn't great. By the time Robert left it was almost bankrupt, and he gathers a fire sale ensued. That strained financial situation meant the company was very keen on keeping legacy hardware alive. "We had this one 2U pizza box server, and one of the three internal fans wore out," Robert explained. The server was so old no replacement was available. "So, we took the lid off and put a bigger fan on top of the component that needed the airflow," Robert confessed. The server couldn't tell the difference and kept working. Six months later, another internal fan broke. "The logical thing to do was to put another ill-fitted fan in/on the server to ensure that the airflow was good," Robert said. Again, the server didn’t have a problem with that. The server, which was an integral part of the company’s daily operations, was now operating with one original fan, no lid, and two non-standard fans precariously balanced over its exposed components. "We had to be careful when working near the server, because too much shaking would cause the fans to fall over," Robert confessed. When the third internal fan died, management finally decided to buy a new box. Have you rigged risky hardware and gotten away with it? If so, share your story by clicking here to send email to Who, Me? Tesco replacing VMware with Proxmox in its stores and on the edge
2026-10-26

Tesco replacing VMware with Proxmox in its stores and on the edge

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Advertises for someone to ‘own cost-effective alternative virtualisation platform’ UK retail giant Tesco appears to have decided to replace VMware with Proxmox in its stores. A job ad for a new Head of Software Engineering: Edge Platform role says the gig involves leading “two major, interconnected engineering priorities: modernising Tesco’s edge virtualisation estate through the migration from VMware to Proxmox, and building a robust, scalable software-driven Edge Management Platform to provision, manage, monitor and maintain the retail edge estate throughout its lifecycle.” The stakes are high because the job ad says Tesco’s Edge Platform “provides the underlying computing environment and software capabilities that enable applications to run across our stores.” “Store technology must remain dependable despite connectivity disruptions, constrained local resources and the complexity of managing software and infrastructure changes across thousands of locations. Failures or poorly executed changes can directly affect store operations, colleague productivity and customer experience.” Whoever scores the job will “own the engineering strategy and execution of Tesco’s migration from VMware to Proxmox across the edge estate, delivering a secure, reliable and cost-effective alternative virtualisation platform” - and do it from Bengaluru, India. One KPI is “Safe and predictable migration from VMware to Proxmox, successful validation of required store configurations, reduced legacy dependencies and effective operational handover.” In case you came in late, Proxmox is an open-source virtualization platform offered by an Austrian company. The product has gained considerable interest as a VMware alternative for edge, hyperconverged, and basic server virtualization scenarios. Proxmox is trying to grow into a full software-defined datacenter contender, too. Independent analyst Michael Warrilow rates [PDF] Proxmox as technically strong but warns would-be users will need to find a support partner because until recently the company only offered support during business hours in Austria. That changed in September when Proxmox announced 24/7 support for enterprise customers. In an email to The Register, Warrilow said “Modernisation is the key to reducing the dependence on VMware: reduce legacy whilst adding incremental value to a new initiative.” “Supermarkets and general merchandise are ripe for this, more so given they are a location where VMware had more competition and less differentiation.” “The Tesco job spec has many of the ingredients for a next-gen, cloud-inspired instore experience,” he added. “API-driven, ‘programmable’ infrastructure that allows rapid deployment and a better experience for customers.” Tesco operates over 5,000 stores around the world. We’ve asked the retail giant to enumerate the extent of its planned Proxmox rollout. Scuttlebutt suggests Tesco will adopt Microsoft’s Hyper-V for its core workloads. VMware, however, has indicated to The Register it does not consider Tesco a lost cause. Tesco is making the move after suing VMware over a contract dispute. The retailer claims VMware is bound to support software it acquired under a perpetual license, and to upgrade some software. The virtualization giant retorted that it no longer makes the software Tesco wants to upgrade to. Tesco says that during negotiations, VMware made an offer that represented a 175 percent price increase. The matter remains before the courts, with hearings likely in 2027. VMware’s dispute with several Siemens companies also continues. In that case, VMware alleged Siemens abused copyright by using more software than its licenses permitted. Siemens’ parent company, Siemens AG, appears to have recently exited the case, an important change as it is Germany-based and the case is being heard in the USA. The remaining defendants last week filed a defence that denies VMware’s claims. In better news for VMware, it last week announced a ten-year agreement with Japanese carrier NTT Docomo and a continued engagement at transport giant FedEx. Both companies will use VMware’s flagship Cloud Foundation private cloud bundle to power their operations. ® Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Aiven hopes to do for LLMs what it does for databases PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good Techies tiptoed around server to keep it working as company sank Whitehall wants agents guarding critical infrastructure as part of a wider £600M spending spree Kernel maintainers stop asking who used AI, as long as the code is good New embedding, parsing, and reranking models promise smarter agentic RAG workflows Containerized datacenters, androids, and telecoms all feature in Berlin keynote interrupted by environmental protesters GPUzilla boasts modular bit barns could speed up deployments from six months to just two weeks PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?'
