MIT Technology Review
Making the case for context-aware AI
2026-10-22

Making the case for context-aware AI

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Enterprise AI can generate recommendations, automate tasks, and help organizations make decisions faster. But without an understanding of how a business actually operates, even powerful models can miss the constraints and relationships that determine whether a decision is the right one. As companies look to move AI from experimentation into everyday operations, the challenge ahead is giving technology the context to act usefully within complex business processes. For Devavrat Shah, chief scientist of enterprise AI at Celonis, that context is central to what he calls “decision intelligence.” AI agents can support individual tasks within larger decision-making processes, he argues, but they need to understand the role each task plays and the business conditions surrounding it. “You can't just do that in a vacuum,” he says. Without the right operational context, agents risk overlooking important constraints, making costly mistakes, or consuming excessive computing resources as they try to work out what they need to know. The solution begins with connecting AI to the processes and data enterprises already use. A contextual layer can help agents understand workflows, operate within defined guardrails, and produce recommendations that people can review and approve. Shah advises organizations to start with a specific business decision, identify its most difficult steps, and test whether AI can improve them. Measuring gains in speed and value can then help determine whether an application is worth scaling. This approach could also change how employees spend their time. Automating repetitive, manual work and coordinating tasks across teams could free people to focus on more complex strategic decisions. Realizing that benefit, however, will require investment in workforce education, so employees can understand AI outputs and their implications instead of just handing decisions over to agents. Looking to the future, Shah sees opportunities extending from routine operational tasks to enterprise-wide planning in areas such as finance, workforce management, marketing, and sales. With the right context, AI could help organizations evaluate options and make consequential decisions more quickly. As Shah puts it, “Now, maybe [resource planning] should not take that much time, and people are actually making the right choices quickly.” This episode of Business Lab is produced in partnership with Celonis. Megan Tatum: From MIT Technology Review, I'm Megan Tatum, and this is Business Lab, the show that helps business leaders make sense of new technologies coming out of the lab and into the marketplace.This podcast is produced in partnership with Celonis.Now, enterprise AI is at something of a crossroads. After years of experimentation, demos, and pilots, leadership teams want clear proof that scaling up will positively impact their bottom line, but many are missing an integral part of the puzzle. By acting without operational context, enterprise AI and AI agents are performing at a significant deficit. They're applying generalized principles to specialized scenarios and coming up short, leaving business leaders understandably unconvinced and AI's potential unrealized.Two words for you: context matters.My guest today is Devavrat Shah, chief scientist of enterprise AI at Celonis.Welcome, Devavrat. Devavrat Shah: Hello, Megan. Thanks for having me here. Megan: Thank you so much for being here with us today. Devavrat, just to start, why is it so crucial that organizations are thinking about operational context as it relates to enterprise AI right now? What makes it such a critical part of that transition I was talking about from experimentation to scale? Devavrat: It's a fantastic question. And as you correctly noted, we're at the cusp of becoming experimental to mainstream as far as AI is concerned. As you know, I've been teaching AI at MIT as a professor for more than the last two decades, so it's no surprise that I'm very bullish about AI's future in enterprises.The way I think about where AI can make the biggest difference in enterprises is in decision making, helping enterprise make decisions, or putting it other way, how do we bring decision intelligence to enterprises? As we think about decisions, how do we make decisions today? Take MIT Tech Review, take Celonis, take MIT, take any large organization. We have different types of decisions we want to make in very broad strokes. There are decisions which are strategic decisions like episodic, other decisions which are operational that need to be done with a high frequency. In either of them, we have some processes in place. You do this first, you do that first, you do that first, you do that first. Many of us help coordinate those things. Many of us play roles within it, what Jensen Huang calls task. And once you get those things done step by step, then you reach decision.Now, it's those tasks, or what I would call roles, that's where AI can bring in an atomic manner agentic power so that we can at least parts of decisions, if not all of the decisions, we can augment support and maybe sometimes when it's a painful automate. And to get to that, to know which role to do, which task to do, we really need to know the context. You can't just do that in a vacuum. And that's why context with AI put together in the right way would bring what I would call decision intelligence to enterprise. And that's where I think there'll be the biggest transformation that would come. Megan: Right. To drill down into that into a bit more detail, we know many organizations are, as you say, embracing identic AI right now with this potential for increasingly autonomous decision making. If context isn't introduced in parallel, what are the commercial risks at play? Devavrat: Fantastic. Let's just take the setting I was describing. We are trying to automate a role or support a role or augment a role depending on the type of decision, depending upon type of task. And let's for a second imagine that all these, as they're called, frontier labs have built these amazing models and amazing AI infrastructure so that everything that's happened in the universe and it's likely to happen in the universe is all there.Now, we have a small decision that we want to make for that very specific context is there. And without having access to that, this massive system, this extremely bright person would figure that out eventually by asking lots of questions, the way we do it as humans, and then finally do the right thing. The problem is to get to that, it will do so much compute at some level that to get simple tasks done, we are spending massive amount of compute. So that's scenario one. In reality, that's not going to be the scenario, and we'll miss some important context, important constraint, important guardrail, and then we'll make a mistake. And sometimes that mistake can be okay. It's like me giving you a recommendation saying, go restaurant A, and maybe you may not enjoy the dinner, versus sometimes mistakes will be very, very expensive. That is me telling you what the financial forecast is and somehow that leads to the company's doom. Megan: Right. So in an enterprise setting, these are really business critical decisions that are being made. So the impact is that much more if we get this wrong, isn't it? And on a practical level then, what are the different ways that organizations can add in this context layer we're talking about? How does Celonis, for example, deliver that operational context via a real time digital twin of an organization? Devavrat: Absolutely. I think if it might be useful just to double click on the history of Celonis. As you know, Celonis, as an organization, came into existence with the mission, with the goal of helping organizations adhere to their ideal processes, build better processes, enable people to work with multiple processes in an intertwined manner. And that's where their primary, let's call it a DNA, has been about what I would call process intelligence. Take how enterprises help put multiple things together, digitize them, enable people to get their processes better, understand where the issues are, and then how do you re-engineer the process within organizations so that things can happen better. Now, if you want to bring decision intelligence into this, each atomic step of this process that was potentially was done before by experts, people, now we want agents to help us enable that. And that's where knowing this context of a process within enterprise and then bringing AI agents along with that, what I would like to call it as a roled agents, exactly provide the right way to bring decision intelligence to enterprises. And this is how Celonis brings context to help enterprise make better decision. Megan: Right. And then in terms of how this works as well, how does the context layer relate to both the layer below, that's the systems of record layer, and also the layer above, that identic layer? Devavrat: That's a fantastic question, Megan. The layer below primarily is, if we call it system of record, primarily it's the workflow tools that are there and then the event logs that they generate around it. The layer above is either business user experts consuming information, or they're consuming that information maybe through agents working on top of that context layer. And this is where the way context model, as Celonis calls it, is designed is to do three things simultaneously. One is to get the right abstraction of the layer below, so that the right pieces of the process can be agentified. Two is to bring the rolled agents for each of these pieces in a structured guardrailed manner. And then the three is to be agent first so that if there are other agentic infrastructure that are already talking to it, they can naturally talk to it so that then they can bring the right output for humans or experts to review, look at it, approve, and maybe then at times automate. Megan: Fantastic. And for all the benefits that we're talking about with this, are there also challenges when it comes to implementing a context layer? For example, do most organizations have the right data in place to provide AI with the context it needs? And if not, I wonder what some of the practical steps are that they might need to take to address that. Devavrat: It's again, a crucial question to make this real. We can imagine an ideal world, but if we cannot reach the ideal world, then it's just ideal. So now in this setting, as we discussed the three layers, one being the layer which is the layer below system of record and workflow tools. In some sense, those things already exist. They don't need to be complete. They don't need to be in the cleanest possible manner. The way context model is designed with agentic's architecture, it manages to connect to those things and make sense of it. And then things on top is very much in control of Celonis's infrastructure, so it's not that complicated. The crucial thing here though is when one goes to enterprise, when one opens up, let's say a system of record or workflow tool, event logs or things like that, one has to go through, what I would call, classical InfoSec to make sure that you can trust a vendor's software that's coming into your organization. And I think that is the primary process that is a step between, before and after, if I may call it. Megan: Right. So that's the real crucial step for people to be taking here. And as AI agents with context are able to make more complex, more accurate hopefully decisions, I wonder what you think the role of human intelligence and input will be moving forward, and how can organizations perhaps strike the balance between the two? Devavrat: There are the benefits and then there are the challenges. Benefits are clear. If you have right AI agents in a right role manner going through your decision processes, hopefully they're doing it quicker and hence your decisions are taken earlier so that your opportunity cost is not lost. You are taking decisions in time, you can plan much better, et cetera, et cetera. This is especially useful when you have to take very manual, repetitive, grungy tasks where access to workforce, coordination between people become crucial parts of the decision process. In a sense, that's where the huge value can come in. The flip side of that is that means that people won't be doing that.On the other hand, the other type of decision that we always make, which are strategic decisions. It could be a small strategic decision, it could be large strategic decision, but the strategic decisions that typically most organizations don't have bandwidth to take to think, that opportunity would open up and that's where likely lots of humans and experts would engage more. In a sense, now they would have better support helping them rationally think through what are the choices that they have, how do different choices compare? And then at the end of the day, it's organization's role to decide which of the option to take because it belongs to their interest in mind where they want to go.And so in a sense, there will be what I would call shift of workforce in terms of instead of doing a lot more repetitive, a lot less repetitive, but a lot more strategic work and that's where people will have to spend time.The other place where I am personally very optimistic is like every technology change creates new work that it's difficult to predict at the time of change. My hope is that there will be another type of opportunity and work that will be created where we'll be engaging our workforce. Goes without saying, this would mean that we will have to carefully think through reeducating or educating some of our workforce thoughtfully. And I think as an academic in me, as an educator in me, I really feel that the timescale at which these changes are happening are so fast. So we need to also act in a similar manner. Megan: For any decision maker listening to this, how should they start implementing these changes? For example, how can they prepare their workforce? What role should governance play as well? Devavrat: Okay, that's a great question. If you want to start, the first thing you need to do is identify a decision where you want to bring intelligence. Let's identify the process around it. Let's identify each step of the process. Let's take the most crucial, difficult step in that. Let's try to bring AI agents to help you to do that. And then from there you go and then let's measure how much speed are we getting, how much value are we getting? And that'll help us understand whether this was worth it. So that's one. Second is governance. Well, we're going to bring AI in this prescriptive manner for each task in a role. That means that for that role, we can tell what it can or cannot do very clearly. And Celonis prescribes certified role agents for these things. This helps you clearly have an understanding of what sort of contract would be for this.And the third thing, which is people, and now this is where it's really important, is a part which gets automated, but then there's a part where experts will be in the loop and this is where they need to understand the outputs of AI, how do you work with that and what its implication would be? And this is where some AI education is needed and then some tool education is needed. Megan: Yes, absolutely. The pace is incredible, isn't it, in this space? And speaking of, I suppose if we could close with a look ahead, given the right context, of course, what are the use cases perhaps for enterprise AI that are on the horizon that you think could be most impactful or perhaps what you're simply most excited about? Devavrat: I think I would categorize them into three types. They're all decision intelligence, decision-making, but there are three types. One, what I would call many repetitive manual labor-intensive, so that's one.Two, what I would call coordination style tasks. A lot of work that we do right now as an information workforce that's spent in coordination, that's why email spreadsheets, et cetera. A lot of that would be done much better, much quicker and less boring, so to speak.And then the third is where, especially as a support to human decision-makers to provide strategic support augmentation, and that's a third type of decisions that would be done. If I think of any planning process in enterprises, so financial planning, workforce planning, marketing, sales, product, anything, all of those are business critical decisions that we make that take annual planning process, take annual time. Now maybe it should not take that much time, and people are actually making the right choices quickly. Megan: That'd be fantastic, wouldn't it? Fantastic. PHP warns of a third PHP2Shell flaw but won’t rush out a fix
2026-10-26

PHP warns of a third PHP2Shell flaw but won’t rush out a fix

The Next Web | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
PHP has warned of a third flaw that could let an attacker run code on a web server, two days after its emergency fixes for the PHP2Shell bugs. This time it will not rush out a fix. In a short advisory on 23 October, PHP said the new bug, CVE-2026-115560, needs an unusual mix of settings and application code. An application would have to switch its session serialize handler to ‘php’ at runtime before starting a session. Session upload progress would also have to be on. Until the next routine release, which will carry the fix, PHP suggests turning off session upload progress. That setting was already a fallback for one of the first two flaws, so admins who applied it need do nothing more. Elizabeth Barron, the PHP Foundation’s executive director, told The Register that the foundation does not expect that setup in practical use. PHP fixed the first two flaws on 21 October, after researchers using AI reported them. The fixes are in versions 8.5.12, 8.4.27, 8.3.36 and 8.2.35. Meanwhile, a Korean-speaking group linked to North Korea is quietly scanning 216 servers for those first two flaws, CYFIRMA said. The threat intelligence firm says the targets include government agencies, banks, airports and online transaction platforms. They sit in the US, Japan, South Korea, India, Australia, the EU and elsewhere. CYFIRMA published its advisory on 23 October and updated it on 24 October. It says the operators work from 21 hijacked machines. It has not confirmed any mass compromise by this group. Its founder and chief executive, Kumar Ritesh, described the method to The Hacker News. “The scanning is deliberately low-volume and paced. Nodes are rotated and abandoned at the first sign of blocking or rate-limiting, so threshold-based alerting is unlikely to catch it.” Kumar Ritesh, CYFIRMA, to The Hacker News The firm ties the operators to North Korea with high confidence. It points to their language, their talk in a closed I2P channel and their targets. It rates their link to the Lazarus Group at moderate confidence. Next, the advisory says, the operators will pick the most promising hosts and decide which public methods are worth adapting. A separate report no longer stands. On 23 October, Palo Alto Networks’ Unit 42 said attackers had used PHP2Shell against a government body in Europe. Within two hours, it said, they reached password hashes and multi-factor authentication secrets. It saw signs that an AI agent was doing the work. On Sunday, Unit 42 retracted that update. “We are unable to confirm that initial access is associated with the specific PHP2Shell CVEs. We have retracted our previous update from October 23.” Unit 42, Palo Alto Networks Attempts still run into the millions, as Cloudflare has reported. Officials in Germany and Japan had already said they saw no confirmed break-ins. As of Friday, nobody had publicly confirmed a successful break-in through PHP2Shell, The Register reported. Barron said this of the first two flaws: “We currently have no evidence of any exploitation of these vulnerabilities on patched PHP versions.” Elizabeth Barron, to The Register The flaws also reach network storage devices, which run PHP in their own software. Synology has shipped updated PHP packages, AppleInsider reported on 22 October. QNAP confirmed a range of affected products and was still working on fixes, with no date given. It reported no attacks on its products. TerraMaster said it was working toward a fix. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. Spain’s opposition posts AI deepfakes of Sánchez’s wife before election

Spain’s opposition posts AI deepfakes of Sánchez’s wife before election

The Next Web | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Pedro Sánchez at the emergency command post in Oliva, Valencia, on 20 October 2026 Spain’s main opposition party has posted a campaign video built with AI deepfakes. In it, likenesses of people linked to Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez’s Socialists appear to confess to wrongdoing. The conservative People’s party (PP) shared it on 21 October, five weeks before the 29 November general election. By Friday the video had nearly 2 million views. Ashifa Kassam reported for the Guardian that it has set off a wider debate in Spain about AI in politics. The PP has defended the video as a way to point out Socialist hypocrisy. An AI version of Sánchez’s wife, Begoña Gómez, talks about getting a university post thanks to her husband’s job. A deepfake of the former Socialist prime minister José Luis Rodríguez Zapatero talks about hiding jewellery in a safe. Both lines refer to ongoing investigations in which Gómez and Zapatero deny any wrongdoing. Other figures appear to admit to soliciting prostitutes and taking kickbacks. The PP posted it on X with the words “Sí, son zurdos” (“Yes, they are left-handed”). That quoted a post in which Sánchez said he was left-handed. An AI label sits at the bottom in tiny, nearly transparent text, the Guardian reported. Partido Popular (@ppopular) October 21, 2026 Óscar López, Spain’s digital transformation minister, called the video “a colossal lie” in an interview with TVE. “It’s very easy for anyone who isn’t used to AI to confuse that video with reality. And call me crazy, but that seems extremely serious to me.” Óscar López, Spain’s digital transformation minister On Friday, Leire Díez filed a court complaint against the PP. She is a former Socialist party member whose likeness appears in the video. She reportedly accuses the party of defamation and digital identity theft, and wants the video taken down at once. Most of the nine legal experts consulted by El País said the video was not illegal. The PP had marked it as AI-made, and its tone was political satire. “Given the context, everyone knows those people didn’t say those things.” Íñigo Navarro, Comillas Pontifical University, to El País Juan Antonio Lascuraín, a criminal law professor at the Autonomous University of Madrid, said legality was only one question. Whether the video was appropriate or ethical was another. José Javier Olivas researches AI bias and disinformation at Spain’s National University for Distance Education. He called the video a “red line” for how campaigns usually run. Putting words in other people’s mouths “qualifies for disinformation”, he told the Guardian. Borja Sémper of the PP told reporters the video was not meant to deceive anyone. “If there had been malicious intent to make people believe that the characters who appear there are real, then we’d have a problem. But everyone can see that this is a way of using digital tools to highlight the hypocrisy in which the Socialist party operates.” Borja Sémper, PP Political deepfakes have surfaced elsewhere too. Brazil set out to define a deepfake before its October vote. Italy’s Giorgia Meloni has filed to trademark her voice. In the UK, Meta’s Oversight Board ordered the company to remove a deepfake of a local councillor. Most polls suggest the PP will win the election but will need the far-right Vox party to form a government, according to the Guardian. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Swedish startup Strawberry launches AI browser for non-technical teams
2026-10-26

Swedish startup Strawberry launches AI browser for non-technical teams

The Next Web | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Strawberry co-founders, from left: Arian Hanifi, Charles Maddock and Sebastian Thunman Most AI assistants live in a chat window. Stockholm-based Strawberry wants them to work where people already do their jobs: inside the browser, with access to the tabs, tools, and accounts they use every day. The startup released version 1.0 of its AI browser today, taking it out of beta and pitching it at workers who do not write code. That includes people in sales, marketing, recruitment and customer service, who often rely on the same repetitive browser-based tasks but lack the time or technical skills to automate them. Strawberry calls its AI assistants “companions”. Users train them to handle specific jobs, and the assistants learn how a team works over time, the company said. The biggest change in version 1.0 is that this knowledge can now be shared. A salesperson, for example, could train a companion to research potential clients, contact them, and enter their details into a sales database. Once trained, the companion can be used by colleagues across the team, without each person having to start from scratch. Companions can also work on a schedule or start when a new email or sales lead arrives. For larger jobs, such as researching 200 companies, one companion can divide the work among several sub-agents running in parallel. “Today the person who is great with Claude keeps all of it on their own laptop,” said Charles Maddock, Strawberry’s chief executive and co-founder. The company wants to make that expertise available to an entire team rather than leave it with the person who knows how to get the most out of an AI tool. Version 1.0 also adds a way to capture video meetings on Zoom, Microsoft Teams, and Google Meet directly from a user’s computer. Because the recording happens locally, no separate bot needs to join the call. Strawberry then turns the conversation into notes and follow-up tasks. The pitch puts Strawberry somewhere between general-purpose AI chatbots such as ChatGPT and Claude, and personal assistants such as Meta’s Muse. Rather than asking employees to switch to another chat app, Strawberry wants its companions to work across the browser-based services they already use. Giving AI assistants access to those services also raises questions about what they can do without human oversight. Strawberry says companions pause for approval before sending emails, publishing posts, or changing records. Passwords remain in the user’s browser, and companies can keep customer data within the EU. The browser is available for macOS and Windows, with a free plan and paid subscriptions starting at $20 a month. Maddock founded Strawberry with chief operating officer Sebastian Thunman and chief technology officer Arian Hanifi. The 12-person startup raised $6 million in October 2025 in a round co-led by General Catalyst and EQT Ventures. The founders of Lovable, Supabase and Hugging Face also invested. Strawberry first opened its beta to 10,000 people who had signed up in advance that October, according to Ny Teknik. Downloads opened to everyone in mid-February. The company’s next challenge is to show that assistants trained by one employee can reliably do useful work for an entire team, without creating more oversight and maintenance than they save. Texas sues OpenAI and Sam Altman over ChatGPT’s safety for children
2026-10-26

Texas sues OpenAI and Sam Altman over ChatGPT’s safety for children

The Next Web | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Ken Paxton at AmericaFest 2025 in Phoenix Texas has taken OpenAI and its chief executive, Sam Altman, to court. The state accuses them of misleading Texans about ChatGPT and of failing to protect the young people who use it. Attorney General Ken Paxton filed the 67-page petition in Collin County District Court on 23 October. Cameron Thompson reported the filing for Courthouse News. The state’s claims rest on the Texas Deceptive Trade Practices Act and the SCOPE Act. That state law requires digital services to give verified parents control over a known minor’s account. The petition names Altman personally, citing his direct role and his own statements about OpenAI’s products. “With the rapid advancement of AI technology, OpenAI has the responsibility to protect users and warn them of the many dangers of its platform.” Ken Paxton, Texas attorney general, in a statement The free version of ChatGPT has no age verification at all, the petition says. The paid version asks users for their age but does not check it. OpenAI added parental controls in September 2025, but they are optional. Parents get alerts only in “limited situations”. The petition also cites Common Sense Media. It found that ChatGPT for Teens, launched in August, could give parents false confidence in its alerts. “Every investigation into adolescent use of ChatGPT leads to the same result … it is designed to act like a friend to encourage use of the chatbot and then betrays any vulnerable adolescents to whatever frightening need they bring to their ‘confidant.’” Texas’s petition Among its examples is Adam Raine, 16, who died by suicide in April 2025 after months of conversations with ChatGPT. His parents sued OpenAI and Altman in California in August 2025. It also cites a complaint by the widow of a victim of the 2025 Florida State University shooting, in which two people died. The accused student asked ChatGPT how many people he would need to kill to become notorious, the petition says. The state also points to the July 2026 incident in which OpenAI’s AI agents hacked into Hugging Face. Texas asks the court to bar OpenAI from offering ChatGPT to minors in the state. It also wants age verification and parental consent for children. And it wants no new AI model released in Texas until a court-approved independent evaluator has reviewed and approved it. The state seeks civil penalties of up to $10,000 per violation and the disgorgement of OpenAI’s assets. Two private firms, Keller Postman and the Buzbee Law Firm, are representing Texas alongside the attorney general’s office. Earlier this month, eight other US states sued OpenAI over harms to children. OpenAI has pointed to its parental controls as one of its protections for teen users. Paxton is the Republican nominee for the US Senate in Texas. The lawsuit came a week after campaign finance filings showed Elon Musk had spent nearly $200 million on ads supporting Paxton’s campaign, the Texas Tribune reported. Musk has a long-running legal fight with Altman. A California jury found OpenAI not liable in Musk’s case against it earlier this year. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Elon Musk says SpaceX has raised $40bn in debt to buy AI chips
2026-10-26

Elon Musk says SpaceX has raised $40bn in debt to buy AI chips

The Next Web | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Elon Musk at VivaTech in Paris, 16 June 2023 SpaceX has secured $40 billion in debt financing to buy graphics processing units (GPUs), the chips used to run AI systems, Elon Musk said in a post on X on Sunday. As of the past few weeks, debt markets have almost entirely shut off access to money for any company that doesn’t have substantial revenue independent of AI data centers. SpaceX closed $40B of debt for GPUs, but we’re investment grade and have Starlink & launch revenue. That means the 200+ neoclouds are going to see guppy-level death and consolidation. Some will make it through, but not many. Musk did not name the lenders or disclose the interest rate, and SpaceX has not published the financing terms. According to Musk, most of the debt is callable, meaning SpaceX can repay it early. In a second post, he said the money would cover chips arriving in the first and second quarters of next year and would not significantly affect spending this quarter. Musk credited SpaceX’s investment-grade credit rating and revenue from Starlink and rocket launches with helping it secure the financing. Earlier this month, the Financial Times reported that Apollo Global Management was expected to lead a financing package worth around $40 billion. The proposed deal would combine approximately $10 billion in bank loans with $30 billion in investment-grade debt. Musk did not confirm whether Sunday’s announcement referred to that same arrangement. He also used the posts to warn about neocloud companies, which rent out AI computing capacity and rely heavily on data centres. “Debt markets have almost entirely shut off access to money” for companies without substantial revenue streams outside AI data centres, Musk wrote. He predicted that many of the more than 200 neocloud companies would fail or merge, naming neither specific targets nor a timeframe. The sector includes Nebius and CoreWeave. Musk added that SpaceX would not acquire any of them. SpaceX absorbed Musk’s AI company xAI and went public in June. The company now generates more revenue from AI than from rocket launches, according to its latest results. Last week, SpaceX reported that revenue rose 161% to $14.58 billion in the quarter ending September 30. AI revenue grew almost tenfold to $7.56 billion over the same period. On the same earnings call, chief financial officer Bret Johnsen said SpaceX expects to spend between $35 billion and $40 billion on equipment and facilities in the current quarter. Instacart is exploring a sale, and Uber has already walked away
2026-10-26

Instacart is exploring a sale, and Uber has already walked away

The Next Web | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Instacart is exploring a sale and has held at least preliminary talks with DoorDash and Uber. Valida Pau, Jessica E. Lessin and Cory Weinberg reported this for The Information on 23 October, citing people familiar with the matter. The grocery delivery company is working with an investment bank to weigh its options, the outlet said. The talks with Uber appear to have fizzled, The Information said later. Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman heard the same from his own source: Uber walked away, and the two sides are no longer in talks. TNW has not independently verified either report. Discussions with other companies are early and may not lead to a deal, according to The Information. Instacart shares rose 9.2% to $56.44 on Friday after the report, Bloomberg said. That gave the company, which trades as Maplebear, a market value of $13.1bn. Uber shares fell 3.4% and DoorDash shares 3.5%. A typical takeover premium of 30% to 40% would value Instacart at roughly $16bn to $17bn, The Information estimated, according to Reuters. Instacart declined to comment to both Bloomberg and Reuters. DoorDash held talks to buy Instacart once before, in 2021, at a valuation of $40bn to $50bn, The Information reported at the time. Worries about antitrust approval under the Biden administration ended them. Instacart was valued at $39bn in a private round that year and went public in 2023. Both possible buyers have been spending since. Uber agreed a $14.8bn Delivery Hero takeover in July. This month it agreed to buy ezCater for $2.3bn. DoorDash bought Deliveroo last year. Either deal could draw antitrust scrutiny, Reuters noted, because it would combine two of the biggest food delivery platforms in the US. Zak Stambor, an analyst at Emarketer, told Reuters why a sale could make sense now. “Instacart risks getting squeezed between retailers building their own digital channels and larger platforms that can bundle grocery with other services, giving consumers more reasons to keep coming back.” Zak Stambor, Emarketer A deal could give Instacart the resources and reach to compete, Stambor said. He called it plausible because the Trump administration has shown “greater willingness” to approve deals, though perhaps at the cost of selling off parts of the business. Amazon is also expanding Amazon Now, which delivers groceries and essentials in 30 minutes or less, Reuters reported. Instacart, for its part, says its AI-built carts beat its average basket. DoorDash chief executive Tony Xu has said AI agents will increase delivery demand. Instacart reports quarterly results on 5 November, and DoorDash on 4 November.
